On the Integral Part of A A -Motivic Cohomology
Quentin Gazda

Abstract




Abstract

The deepest arithmetic invariants attached to an algebraic variety defined over a number field
F
are conjecturally captured by the
integral part
of its
motivic cohomology
. There are essentially two ways of defining it when
X
is a smooth projective variety: one is via the
K
-theory of a regular integral model, the other is through its
\ell
-adic realization. Both approaches are conjectured to coincide.

This paper initiates the study of motivic cohomology for global fields of positive characteristic, hereafter named
A
-motivic cohomology
, where classical mixed motives are replaced by mixed Anderson
A
-motives. Our main objective is to set the definitions of the
integral part
and the
good
\ell
-adic part
of the
A
-motivic cohomology using Gardeyn’s notion of
maximal models
as the analogue of regular integral models of varieties. Our main result states that the
integral part
is contained in the
good
\ell
-adic part
. As opposed to what is expected in the number field setting, we show that the two approaches do not match in general.

We conclude this work by introducing the submodule of
regulated extensions
of mixed Anderson
A
-motives, for which we expect the two approaches to match, and solve some particular cases of this expectation.

1
Introduction

1.1
The number field picture

The idea of
mixed motives
and
motivic cohomology
has been gradually formulated by Deligne, Beilinson and Lichtenbaum and aims to extend Grothendieck’s philosophy of pure motives. Before discussing the function fields side, subject of the paper, let us first present the classical setting to derive some motivations.

The theory, mostly conjectural, starts with a number field
F
. The hypothetical landscape portrays a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear Tannakian category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
of
mixed motives
over
F
, equipped with several
realization functors
having
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
as source (see
[
Del
, §1]
). Among them, the
\ell
-adic realization functor

V_{\ell}
, for a prime number
\ell
, takes values in the category of continuous
\ell
-adic representations of the absolute Galois group
G_{F}=\operatorname{Gal}(\bar{F}|F)
, given a fixed algebraic closure
\bar{F}
of
F
.

It is expected that
reasonable
cohomology theories factor through the category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
. For instance, the
\ell
-adic realization should recover the étale cohomology of algebraic varieties with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
in the following way: for all integer
i
, one foresees the existence of a functor
h^{i}
, from the category of algebraic varieties over
F
to
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
, making the following diagram of categories commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{Varieties}/F\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}(G_{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\mapsto\operatorname{H}^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X\times_{F}\bar{F}{,}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\ell}}

According to Deligne
[
Del
, §1.3]
, the category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
should admit a
weight filtration
in the sense of Jannsen
[
Jan
, Def 6.3]
, which would coincide with the classical weight filtration of varieties. The
weights
of a mixed motive
M
would then be defined as the breaks of its weight filtration.

From the Tannakian formalism,
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
admits a tensor operation extending the fiber product on varieties. We fix
\mathbbm{1}
a neutral object. Let
M
be a mixed motive over
F
. According to Beilinson
[
Bei1
, §0.3]
(see also
[
YAnd
, Def 17.2.11]
), the
motivic cohomology of
M
is defined as the complex

\operatorname{RHom}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)

in the derived category of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces. Its
i
th cohomology is the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)
, the space of
i
-fold extensions of
\mathbbm{1}
by
M
in
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
. We quote from
[
Sch
, §2]
and
[
Del
, §1.3]
respectively:

Conjecture
.

We expect that:

(C
1
)

for
i\not\in\{0,1\}
,
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)=0
,

(C
2
)

if
w
denotes the smallest weight of
M
and
w\geq 0
, then
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)=0
.

Let us focus on the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space of
1
-fold extensions
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)
. A subspace thereof of fundamental importance is the space of
extensions having everywhere good reduction
. In the literature, we encounter two definitions which are expected to give the same result. Let us first describe its local constructions.

Via the
\ell
-adic realization:

Let
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the local field of
F
at a finite place
\mathfrak{p}
, and let
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
be a mixed motive over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Let
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the absolute Galois group of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and let
I_{\mathfrak{p}}
be its inertia subgroup. Given a prime number
\ell
, one predicts that the
\ell
-adic realization
V_{\ell}
is an exact functor. This allows one to construct a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear morphism, called
the
\ell
-adic realization map of
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

r_{M,\ell,\mathfrak{p}}:\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},M_{\mathfrak{p}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{\mathfrak{p}},V_{\ell}M_{\mathfrak{p}})

which maps the class of an exact sequence
[E_{\mathfrak{p}}]:0\to M_{\mathfrak{p}}\to E_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}}\to 0
in
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
to the class of the continuous cocycle
c:G_{\mathfrak{p}}\to V_{\ell}M_{\mathfrak{p}}
associated to the class of the exact sequence
[V_{\ell}E_{\mathfrak{p}}]:0\to V_{\ell}M_{\mathfrak{p}}\to V_{\ell}E_{\mathfrak{p}}\to V_{\ell}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}}\to 0
in
\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}(G_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

Suppose
\ell
does not divide
\mathfrak{p}
. Following Scholl
[
Sch
]
, we say that
[E_{\mathfrak{p}}]\in\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}(\mathbbm{1},M)
has
good reduction
if
r_{M,\ell,\mathfrak{p}}([E_{\mathfrak{p}}])
splits as a representation of
I_{\mathfrak{p}}
(that is,
[V_{\ell}E_{\mathfrak{p}}]
is zero in
\operatorname{H}^{1}(I_{\mathfrak{p}},V_{\ell}M_{\mathfrak{p}})
). In
[
Sch
, §2 Rmk]
, Scholl conjectures:

Conjecture
.

We expect that:

(C
3
)

The property that
[E_{\mathfrak{p}}]
has good reduction is independent of the prime
\ell
.

We then define
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1},M_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}
as the subspace of
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M_{\mathfrak{p}})
consisting of extensions having good reduction. By
(C

3

)
, it should not depend on
\ell
: this is the
\mathfrak{p}
-integral part
of the motivic cohomology of
\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Via the
K
-theory of regular models:

An other conjectural way of defining the
\mathfrak{p}
-integral part
of motivic cohomology uses its expected link with
K
-theory. Following Beilinson
[
Bei1
]
in the case where
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
is of the form
h^{i-1}(X)(n)
for a smooth projective variety
X
over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
and two integers
n
,
i\geq 1
, there should be a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces (see
loc. cit.
for details):

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}(\mathbbm{1},M_{\mathfrak{p}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(K_{2n-i}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})^{(n)}.

(1.1)

Assume that
X
has a regular model
\mathscr{X}
over
\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
(
i.e.

\mathscr{X}
is regular over
\operatorname{Spec}\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\mathscr{X}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\operatorname{Spec}F_{\mathfrak{p}}=X
). Then, we define
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1},M_{\mathfrak{p}})
to be the inverse image of

\operatorname{image}\left((K_{2n-i}(\mathscr{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})^{(n)}\longrightarrow(K_{2n-i}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})^{(n)}\right)

through (
1.1
). By
[
Bei1
, Lem. 8.3.1]
, this does not depend on the choice of the model
\mathscr{X}
. The next conjecture supersedes
(C

3

)
:

Conjecture
.

We expect that

(C
4
)

For any prime
\ell
not under
\mathfrak{p}
, we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1},M_{\mathfrak{p}})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1},M_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}
.

The global version of the integral part of the motivic cohomology of a mixed motive
M
over
F
is recovered as follows. The motive
M
induces a motive
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
by localization. Assuming conjecture
(C

3

)
, we say that an extension
[E]
(of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{M}
) has
everywhere good reduction
if, for all
\mathfrak{p}
, the extension
[E_{\mathfrak{p}}]
belongs to
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},M_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}
for some prime
\ell
not dividing
\mathfrak{p}
. We denote by
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1},M)
the subspace of
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)
consisting of extensions having everywhere good reduction.

Similarly, in the case where
M=h^{i-1}(X)(n)
for a smooth projective variety
X
over
F
, we let
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)
be the subspace of extensions
[E]
such that
[E_{\mathfrak{p}}]
belongs to
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},M_{\mathfrak{p}})
for all finite places
\mathfrak{p}
of
F
. In virtue of the previous conjectures, we should have:

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1},M)=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)\cong(K_{2n-i}(\mathscr{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})^{(n)},

where
\mathscr{X}
is a regular model of
X
over
\pazocal{O}_{F}
.

The space
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)
is at the heart of Beilinson’s conjectures, the next expectation being the starting point thereof:

Conjecture
.

We expect that

(C
5
)

The space
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},M)
has finite dimension over
\mathbb{Q}
.

1.2
The function field picture

Despite its intrinsic obscurities, motivic cohomology remains a difficult subject also because its definition sits on a completely conjectural framework. The present paper grew out as an attempt to understand the analogous picture in function field arithmetic. There, the theory looks more promising using
Anderson
A
-motives
, instead of classical motives, whose definition is well-established. This parallel has been drawn by many authors and led to celebrated achievements. The analogue of the Tate conjecture
[
Tag
]

[
Tam
]
, of Grothendieck’s periods conjecture
[
Pap
]
and of the Hodge conjecture
[
HaJu
]
are now theorems on the function fields side. The recent volume
[
tMo
]
records some of these feats. Counterparts of Motivic cohomology in function field arithmetic have not been studied yet, although recent works of Taelman
[
Tae2
]

[
Tae4
]
and Mornev
[
Mor1
]
strongly suggest the pertinence of such a project.

The setting

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finite field,
q
its number of elements, and let
(C,\pazocal{O}_{C})
be a geometrically irreducible smooth projective curve over
\mathbb{F}
. We let
K
be the function field of
C
and we fix a closed point
\infty
on
C
. The
\mathbb{F}
-algebra:

A:=\pazocal{O}_{C}(C\setminus\{\infty\})

has
K
as its field of fractions. We let
K_{\infty}
be the completion of
K
with respect to the valuation
v_{\infty}
associated to
\infty
, and let
K_{\infty}^{s}
be the separable closure of
K_{\infty}
. The analogy with number fields that guide us in this text is:

Number fields:

\mathbb{Z}

\subset

\mathbb{Q}

\subset

\mathbb{R}

\subset

\mathbb{C}

\wr

\wr

\wr

\wr

Function fields:

A

\subset

K

\subset

K_{\infty}

\subset

K_{\infty}^{s}

Let
R
be an
A
-algebra. The analogy with number fields disappears when one considers the tensor product
A\otimes R
, which is at the heart of the definition of Anderson
A
-motives (unlabeled fiber and tensor products are over
\mathbb{F}
). We consider the ring endomorphism
\tau
of
A\otimes R
which acts as the identity on
A
and as the
q
-Frobenius on
R
. We let
\mathfrak{j}
be the ideal of
A\otimes R
generated by the set
\{a\otimes 1-1\otimes a|a\in A\}
.

Following
[
GAnd
]
, an
Anderson
A
-motive
\underline{M}
over
R
is a pair
(M,\tau_{M})
where
M
denotes a finite projective
A\otimes R
-module of constant rank, and where
\tau_{M}:(\tau^{*}M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\to M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
is an
(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
-linear isomorphism (Definition
2.3
). We let
\mathscr{M}_{R}
denote the category of Anderson
A
-motives with obvious morphisms.
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is known to be
A
-linear, rigid monoidal, and is exact in the sense of Quillen (Proposition
2.15
) but not abelian (
[
HaJu
, §2.3]
or Subsection
2.1
). Let
\mathbbm{1}
in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
be a neutral object for the tensor operation.

Extensions of
A
-motives

The category
\mathscr{M}_{R}
, or rather full subcategories of it, will play the role of the category of Grothendieck’s motives. Guided by this, the next theorem already describes the analogue of motivic cohomology in an explicit manner and is the starting point of our research (see Theorem
2.21
). Let
\underline{M}
be an
A
-motive over
R
.

Theorem A
.

The cohomology of the complex
\left[M\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M}}M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\right]
of
A
-modules, sitting in degrees zero and one, computes the extension modules
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
for all
i
.

We immediately deduce that
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
vanishes for
i>1
. For
i=1
, the
A
-module of degree one extensions admit the following explicit description. There is a natural surjective morphism

\iota:M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})

(1.2)

which maps
m\in M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
to the class of the extension of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{M}
whose middle term is the
A
-motive given by
\left[M\oplus(A\otimes R),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tau_{M}&m\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]
(Subsection
2.2
). The kernel of
\iota
being
(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M)
, we recover the isomorphism provided by Theorem
A
.

Remark
.

Extension groups in the full subcategory of
\mathscr{M}_{R}
consisting of
effective
A
-motives
(see Definition
2.5
) were already determined in the existing literature (see
e.g.

[
Tae3
]
,
[
Tae4
]
,
[
PaRa
]
). The novelty of Theorem
A
is to consider the whole category
\mathscr{M}_{R}
.

To pursue the analogy with number fields, we now present the notion of
weights
and
mixedness
for Anderson
A
-motives over fields. In the case
A=\mathbb{F}[t]
or
\deg(\infty)=1
and over a complete algebraically closed base field, the corresponding definitions were carried out respectively by Taelman
[
Tae1
]
and Hartl–Juschka
[
HaJu
]
. We completed this picture in the most general way (over any
A
-field and without any restriction on
\deg(\infty)
).

Let
R=F
be a field. To an Anderson
A
-motive
\underline{M}
over
F
, we attach an
isocrystal

\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})
at
\infty
(in the sense of
[
Mor2
]
). The naming
isocrystal
is borrowed from
p
-adic Hodge theory, where the function field setting allows to apply the non-archimedean theory at the infinite point
\infty
of
C
as well. Following
[
GAnd
, 1.9]
, we call
\underline{M}

pure
of weight
\mu
if its associated isocrystal is pure of slope
-\mu
(Definition
3.21
). More generally, we call
\underline{M}

mixed
if there exists rational numbers
\mu_{1}<\ldots<\mu_{s}
together with a finite ascending filtration of
\underline{M}
by saturated sub-
A
-motives:

0=W_{\mu_{0}}\underline{M}\subsetneq W_{\mu_{1}}\underline{M}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq W_{\mu_{s}}\underline{M}=\underline{M}

for which the successive quotients
W_{\mu_{i}}\underline{M}/W_{\mu_{i-1}}\underline{M}
are pure of weight
\mu_{i}
(Definition
3.23
). We show in Proposition
3.28
that such a filtration–when it exists–is unique, as well as the numbers
\mu_{i}
which are then called the
weights
of
\underline{M}
. As it was observed by Hartl–Juschka (
[
HaJu
, Ex. 2.3.13]
), there exist non mixed
A
-motives. Nonetheless, it is always possible to define the weights of a (not necessarily mixed)
A
-motive via the Dieudonné-Manin decomposition of isocrystals (see Definition
3.20
). We denote by
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
the full subcategory of
\mathscr{M}_{F}
whose objects are mixed Anderson
A
-motives over
F
. The main results of Section
3
are gathered in the next theorem.

Theorem B
.

Let
\underline{M}
be an object of
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
. If all the weights of
\underline{M}
are non-positive, then every extension of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{M}
is mixed, that is:

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M}).

If all the weights of
\underline{M}
are positive, then an extension of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{M}
is mixed if and only if its class is torsion, that is:

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})^{\operatorname{tors}}.

Furthermore, for
i>1
,
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
is a torsion module for all
\underline{M}
.

Remark
.

For our analogy to be complete, one would rather seek for a
K
-linear category : where
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
is
A
-linear, the classical category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-linear. To obtain a
K
-linear category out of
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
, one introduces
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{F}
–the category of
mixed
A
-motives up to isogenies
–whose objects are the ones of
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
and whose Hom-spaces are given by
{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(-,-)\otimes_{A}K}
(
e.g.

[
Har3
]
,
[
HaJu
]
). Theorem
B
implies that
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=0
for
i>1
and
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=0
if the weights of
\underline{M}
are positive. This reveals that the analogue of the number fields conjecture
(C

1

)
and
(C

2

)
are true for function fields. Note, however, that contrary to what is expected for number fields, the full subcategory of pure
A
-motives is not semi-simple. Hence, we cannot expect any
1
-fold Yoneda extension of two pure
A
-motives to split, even if they have the same weight.

The good
\ell
-adic part

To present the theory of good and integral extensions, we now assume that
F
is a finite field extension of
K
(namely, a
global function field
). Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a finite place of
F
(
i.e.
not above
\infty
),
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
the associated local function field,
F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
a separable closure of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
G_{\mathfrak{p}}=\operatorname{Gal}(F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}|F_{\mathfrak{p}})
the absolute Galois group of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
equipped with the profinite topology. Given a maximal ideal
\ell
in
A
which does not lie under
\mathfrak{p}
, there is an
\ell
-adic realization functor
from
\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
to the category of continuous
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-linear representations of
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Given an object
\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}}=(M_{\mathfrak{p}},\tau_{M})
of
\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, it is defined as the
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-module

\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\varprojlim_{n}~\{m\in(M_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s})/\ell^{n}(M_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s})~|~m=\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}m)\}

where
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
acts compatibly on the right of the tensor
M_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
(Definition
2.24
).

We prove in Corollary
2.28
that
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}
is exact. This paves the way for introducing
extensions with good reduction
, as Scholl did in the number fields setting. Let
I_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset G_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the inertia subgroup. We consider the
\ell
-adic realization map
restricted to
I_{\mathfrak{p}}
:

r_{\underline{M},\ell,\mathfrak{p}}:\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{1}(I_{\mathfrak{p}},\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})

(1.3)

(we refer to Subsection
2.3
). Mimicking Scholl’s approach, we say that an extension
[\underline{E}_{\mathfrak{p}}]
of
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}}
by
\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}}
has
good reduction
if
[\underline{E}_{\mathfrak{p}}]
lies in the kernel of (
1.3
). As in the number field setting, we expect this definition to be independent of
\ell
, although this is presumably out of our reach. We let
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}
denote the kernel of
r_{\underline{M},\ell,\mathfrak{p}}
(Definition
2.30
).

The integral part

Gardeyn in
[
Gar2
]
has introduced a notion of
maximal models
for
\tau
-sheaves. Inspired by Gardeyn’s work, we developed the notion of
maximal integral models of
A
-motives
(Section
4
). They form the function field analogue of Néron models of abelian varieties, or more generally, of regular models of varieties.

Let
\underline{M}
be an
A
-motive over
F
, and denote by
\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}}
the
A
-motive of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
obtained from
\underline{M}
by base-change along
F\subset F_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Let
\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the valuation ring of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
and let also
\pazocal{O}_{F}
denote the integral closure of
A
in
F
.

Definition

(Definition
4.19
)
.

Let
L
be a finitely generated sub-
A\otimes\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
-module of
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
(resp.
A\otimes\pazocal{O}_{F}
-module of
M
).

(1)

We say that
L
is an
\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
-
model for
\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}}
if it generates
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\tau_{M_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\tau^{*}L)\subset L[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
.

(2)

We say that
L
is an
\pazocal{O}_{F}
-
model for
\underline{M}
if it generates
M
over
F
and
\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}L)\subset L[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
.

We say that
L
is
maximal
if
L
is not strictly contained in any other model.

As opposed to
[
Gar2
, Def 2.1 & 2.3]
, we do not ask for an
\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
-model (resp.
\pazocal{O}_{F}
-model) to be locally free. We show that this is automatic for maximal ones (Theorem
4.32
). Compared to Gardeyn, our exposition is therefore simplified and avoids the use of a technical lemma due to L. Lafforgue
[
Gar2
, §2.2]
. Our next result should be compared with
[
Gar2
, Prop 2.13]
(see Proposition
4.51
and Theorems
4.53
and
4.55
in the text).

Theorem C
.

A maximal
\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
-model
M_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}
for
\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}}
(resp.
\pazocal{O}_{F}
-model
M_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}
for
\underline{M}
) exists and is unique. It is locally free over
A\otimes\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
(resp.
A\otimes\pazocal{O}_{F}
). In addition, the maximal local and global models are related by canonical isomorphisms:

M_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}\cong\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}}{(M\cap M_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}})},\quad\text{and}\quad M_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cong M_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}\otimes_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}},

where the intersection is taken over finite places
\mathfrak{p}
of
F
.

Along the way, we also prove a good reduction criterion for
A
-motives, in the style of Néron-Ogg-Shafarevič (Proposition
4.49
).

Then, we call
\mathfrak{p}
-integral
any extension of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{M}
which arises as an element of
\iota(M_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}])
(
1.2
). We let
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})
be the module of
\mathfrak{p}
-integral extensions (Definition
4.58
). Our main result (repeated from Theorem
4.60
) is the next:

Theorem D
.

Let
\ell
be a maximal ideal of
A
which does not lie under
\mathfrak{p}
. Then
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})
is a sub-
A
-module of
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{good}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}
.

Surprisingly enough, we cannot claim equality in general. In Subsection
5.1
, in the simplest case of the neutral
A
-motive, we construct for some
\ell
and
\mathfrak{p}
an explicit extension in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}
which does not belong to
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

In Subsection
4.4
, we define the
global
version of the above. Namely, the
A
-motive
\underline{M}
defines an
A
-motive
\underline{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
by extending the base field. We let
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
be the module of
integral extensions
;
i.e.
of extensions that are
\mathfrak{p}
-integral for all
\mathfrak{p}
after base-change along
F\subset F_{\mathfrak{p}}
(Definition
4.58
). Our second main result (repeated from Theorem
4.62
) is the following:

Theorem E
.

The
A
-module
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
equals the image of
M_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
through
\iota
. In addition,
\iota
induces a natural isomorphism of
A
-modules:

\frac{M_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}{(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M_{\pazocal{O}_{F}})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M}).

Regulated extensions of
A
-motives

We are facing two main issues to pursue our analogy: the counterpart of Conjecture
(C

4

)
does not hold true and, more seriously, neither is the counterpart of
(C

5

)
: the
A
-module
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
is typically not finitely generated. Those facts suggest that the category
\mathscr{M}_{F}
–and also
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
–is
too huge
to held a convincing motivic cohomology theory. We end this text by presenting a conjectural picture aiming to answer the analogue of
(C

4

)
and
(C

5

)
.

Pink, in the context of function fields Hodge structures
[
Pin
, §6]
, is facing a similar issue. There, he introduced the notion of
Hodge additivity
, whose counter-part for
A
-motives is as follow.

Definition

(
cf
.
5.2
for details)
.

Let
0\to\underline{M}\to\underline{E}\to\underline{N}\to 0
be an exact sequence of
A
-motives over
F
. We say that
[\underline{E}]
is
regulated
if the Hodge polygon of the Hodge-Pink structure attached to
\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N}
matches the one of
\underline{E}
(see
[
Pin
, §6]
). We denote by
\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}
the submodule of regulated extensions.

Pink’s intuition for introducing
Hodge additivity
is driven by the inexact feature of the operation assigning to a Hodge-Pink structure its ”classical” Hodge structure (
i.e.
the data of its Hodge filtration). Although Pink is concerned with computation of Hodge groups, for our purpose this prevents the well-definedness of a
regulator map
from the module of extensions of
A
-motives to that of extensions of ”classical” Hodge structures (see Remark
5.2
).
Regulated extensions
are designed to resolve this point, hence the naming. The notion of
regulation
has applications in sequels to this text
[
Gaz
]
,
[
GaMa
]
.

In Corollary
5.5
, we prove that
\iota
induces an isomorphism of
A
-modules:

\frac{M+\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}M)}{(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M}).

While
\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\pazocal{O}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
is still not finitely generated over
A
in general, a version of Conjecture
(C

5

)
involving the infinite places holds (we refer the reader to
[
Gaz
, Thm. 4.1]
). Concerning the analogue of Conjecture
(C

4

)
, we strongly suspect regulated extensions to also correct the integral
\neq
good extensions phenomenon; namely we expect the following to hold for
A
-motives
\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}}
over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
(see Conjecture
5.8
):

Conjecture
.

Let
\ell
be a maximal ideal of
A
not under
\mathfrak{p}
. Then,

\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}.

In particular, the module
\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}
does not depend on
\ell
.

We conclude this text by solving particular instances of the above conjecture (Subsection
5.3
). The general case, however, remains open.

Acknowledgments:

The main results presented in this text were obtained during the author’s PhD thesis. I would like to express my profound gratitude to Professors Gebhard Böckle and Urs Hartl, as well as my advisor, Professor Federico Pellarin. I am greatly indebted to the anonymous referee for their advice and suggestions, which significantly contributed to improving the quality of this document. Among the suggestions made by the referee, I am especially grateful for their assistance in completing the original proof of Theorem
5.13
and resolving the above conjecture for positive Carlitz twists
\underline{A}(n)
. Our initial argument only applied to cases where
n
was a power of the characteristic of
\mathbb{F}
. Parts of this text were revised during my time at the Max Planck Institute for Mathematics in Bonn, and I am thankful for their hospitality and financial support.

1.3
Plan of the paper

The paper is organized as follows.

In the beginning of Section
2
, Subsection
2.1
, we review the usual set up (notations, definitions, basic properties) of
A
-motives over an arbitrary commutative and unital
A
-algebra. We follow
[
HaJu
]
and
[
Har3
]
as a guideline, though the former reference is concerned with the particular choice of a closed point
\infty
of degree one and over a complete algebraically closed field. Most of the results on
A
-motives extend without changes to our more general setting. In Subsection
2.2
,
A
-Motivic cohomology is introduced. We describe the extension modules in categories of
A
-motives and obtain Theorem
A
as Theorem
2.21
in the text. In Subsection
2.3
, we recall the definition and main properties of the
\ell
-adic realization functor for
A
-motives, and introduce
extensions having good reduction with respect to
\ell
in Definition
2.30
.

Section
3
is concerned with mixed
A
-motives. In the beginning of Subsection
3.1
we recall, and add some new material, to the theory of function fields isocrystals in the steps of
[
Mor2
]
. The main ingredient, used later on in Subsection
3.1
to define the category of mixed
A
-motives over
A
-fields, is the existence and uniqueness of the slope filtration (extending
[
Har2
, Prop. 1.5.10]
to general coefficient rings
A
). We focus on extension modules in the category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
in Subsection
3.2
where we deduce Theorem
B
from Propositions
3.37
,
3.39
and Theorem
3.42
.

In Section
4
, we develop the notion of
maximal integral models
of
A
-motives over local and global function fields. It splits into four subsections. In Subsection
4.1
, we present integral models of
Frobenius spaces
over local function fields. The theory is much easier than the one for
A
-motives, introduced over a local function field in Subsection
4.2
and over a global function field in Subsection
4.3
. Although our definition of integral model is inspired by Gardeyn’s work in the context of
\tau
-sheaves
[
Gar2
]
, our presentation is simpler as we removed the
locally free
assumption. That maximal integral models are locally free is automatic, as we show in Theorems
4.32
and
4.53
. The chief aim of this section, however, is Subsection
4.4
where we use the results of the earlier subsections to prove Theorems
D
and
E
(respectively Theorems
4.60
and
4.62
in the text).

In our last Section
5
, we introduce the notion of
regulated extensions of
A
-motives
with an eye toward understanding the lack of equality in Theorem
D
, highlighted in Subsection
5.1
. We recall the definition of Hodge polygons, as introduced in
[
Pin
]
, in Subsection
5.2
. Those are used to define regulated extensions in Definition
5.1
. We conclude this text by Subsection
5.3
, where we present a general hope that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})
and
\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{M}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\ell}
match. We then prove some particular instances of this expectation, namely when
\underline{M}
arises from a Frobenius space with good reduction (Theorem
5.9
), or when
\underline{M}
is a positive Carlitz’s twist
\underline{A}(n)
for
n\geq 0
(Theorem
5.13
).

All along this text, we make constant use of extension modules in exact but non necessarily abelian categories. Although everything works as expected, we decided to add an appendix on exact categories as Section
A
because we did not find all of the relevant references in the literature.

2
Anderson
A
-motives and their extension modules

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finite field with
q
elements. By convention, unlabeled tensor products and fiber products throughout this text are over
\mathbb{F}
; all algebras are associative, commutative and with unit. Let
(C,\pazocal{O}_{C})
be a geometrically irreducible smooth projective curve over
\mathbb{F}
, and fix a closed point
\infty
on
C
. Let
A:=\pazocal{O}_{C}(C\setminus\{\infty\})
be the ring of regular functions on
C\setminus\{\infty\}
and let
K
be the function field of
C
.

2.1
Definition of
A
-motives

This subsection is devoted to define and recall the main properties of Anderson
A
-motives. We begin with a paragraph of notations.

Preliminaries on
\mathfrak{j}
and
\tau

Let
R
be an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra and let
\kappa:A\to R
be an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra morphism.
R
will be referred to as
the base algebra
and
\kappa
as the
characteristic morphism
. The kernel of
\kappa
is called
the characteristic of
(R,\kappa)
. We consider the ideal
\mathfrak{j}=\mathfrak{j}_{\kappa}
of
A\otimes R
generated by the set
\{a\otimes 1-1\otimes\kappa(a)|a\in A\}
;
\mathfrak{j}
is equivalently defined as the kernel of
A\otimes R\to R
,
a\otimes f\mapsto\kappa(a)f
. The following observation appears in
[
Har3
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

The ideal
\mathfrak{j}
is an invertible
A\otimes R
-module.

According to Lemma
2.1
, the
A\otimes R
-module
\mathfrak{j}^{n}
makes sense for any integer
n
and we have canonical inclusions
\mathfrak{j}^{-n}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{j}^{-(n+1)}
. Given an
A\otimes R
-module
M
, we denote by
M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
the colimit

M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]:=\varinjlim~M\otimes_{A\otimes R}\mathfrak{j}^{-n}

taken over non-negative integers
n
in the category of
A\otimes R
-modules.

Remark 2.2
.

For consistency with
[
Har3
]
, observe that the map of schemes:

D(\mathfrak{j})=(\operatorname{Spec}A\otimes R)\setminus V(\mathfrak{j})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}],\quad\mathfrak{p}\longmapsto\mathfrak{p}[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}],

composed with
\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\to\operatorname{Spec}A\otimes R
,
\mathfrak{q}\mapsto\mathfrak{q}\cap(A\otimes R)
, coincides with the canonical inclusion
D(\mathfrak{j})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A\otimes R
of an open subscheme. Hence,
D(\mathfrak{j})
and
\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
are canonically isomorphic. As tensor products commute with direct limits, we further have:

M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\cong M\otimes_{A\otimes R}(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]

which implies that
M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
is obtained by pulling back
M
along the open immersion

(\operatorname{Spec}A\otimes R)\setminus V(\mathfrak{j})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A\otimes R.

Given another module
N
, we shall use freely the identifications
1
1

1

For any integer
n
, the module
\mathfrak{j}^{n}
is finite projective and hence
\operatorname{Hom}(N,M)\otimes\mathfrak{j}^{n}\cong\operatorname{Hom}(N,M\otimes\mathfrak{j}^{n})
; the first isomorphism follows as direct limits commute with
\operatorname{Hom}(N,-)
. The second one follows from
\operatorname{Hom}(N\otimes\mathfrak{j}^{-n},M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}])\cong\operatorname{Hom}(N,M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\otimes\mathfrak{j}^{n})
together with
M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\otimes\mathfrak{j}^{n}\cong M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
(as tensor products commute with colimits).

\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(N,M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(N,M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}_{(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}(N[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}],M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]).

We shall also use, without further notice, that the map
A\otimes R\to(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
is flat
2
2

2

This follows from the flatness of the
A\otimes R
-module
\mathfrak{j}^{n}
together with the fact that colimits preserve exact sequences.
.

We denote by
\tau:A\otimes R\to A\otimes R
be the
A
-linear morphism given by
a\otimes r\mapsto a\otimes r^{q}
on elementary tensors. We denote by
\tau^{*}M
be the pull-back of
M
by
\tau
(
[
Bou
, A.II.§5]
);
i.e.

\tau^{*}M
is the
A\otimes R
-module

\tau^{*}M:=(A\otimes R)\otimes_{\tau,A\otimes R}M

where the subscript
\tau
signifies that the relation
(a\otimes_{\tau}bm)=(a\tau(b)\otimes_{\tau}m)
holds for all elements
a,b\in A\otimes R
,
m\in M
, and where the module structure corresponds to
b\cdot(a\otimes_{\tau}m):=(ba\otimes_{\tau}m)
. We let

\mathbf{1}:\tau^{*}(A\otimes R)\longrightarrow A\otimes R,

be the
A\otimes R
-linear morphism which maps
(a\otimes r)\otimes_{\tau}(b\otimes s)\in\tau^{*}(A\otimes R)
to
ab\otimes rs^{q}\in A\otimes R
.

Anderson
A
-motives

The next definition takes its roots in the work of Anderson
[
GAnd
]
, though this version is borrowed from
[
Har3
, Def. 2.1]
:

Definition 2.3
.

An
Anderson
A
-motive
\underline{M}
(over
R
)
is a pair
(M,\tau_{M})
where
M
is a projective
A\otimes R
-module of finite constant rank and where
\tau_{M}:(\tau^{*}M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\to M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
is an isomorphism of
(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
-modules.

In all the following, we shall simply write
A
-motive
instead of
Anderson
A
-motive
. The
rank of
\underline{M}
is the (constant) rank of
M
over
A\otimes R
.

A morphism
(M,\tau_{M})\to(N,\tau_{N})
of
A
-motives (over
R
) is an
A\otimes R
-linear morphism
{f:M\to N}
such that
f\circ\tau_{M}=\tau_{N}\circ\tau^{*}f
. We let
\mathscr{M}_{R}
be the
A
-linear category of
A
-motives over
R
.

Remark 2.4
.

A
-motives as in Definition
2.3
are called
abelian
A
-motives
by several authors (see
e.g.

[
BrPa
]
). The word
abelian
refers to the assumption that the underlying
{A\otimes R}
-module is finite projective. Dropping this assumption is not a good strategy in our work, as too many analogies with number fields motives would fail to hold.

Definition 2.5
.

An
A
-motive
\underline{M}=(M,\tau_{M})
over
R
is called
effective
if
\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}M)\subset M
. We let
\mathscr{M}_{R}^{\text{eff}}
be the full subcategory of
\mathscr{M}_{R}
whose objects are effective
A
-motives.

Given an
A
-motive
\underline{M}
, we will write
M
without an underline to refer to its underlying module and write
\tau_{N}
to refer to the underlying morphism. We list below some important classical constructions:

-

Let
\mathbbm{1}
be the
unit
A
-motive over
R
defined as
(A\otimes R,\textbf{1})
.

-

The
direct sum
of two
A
-motives
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
, denoted
\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N}
, is representable in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
and corresponds to the
A
-motive whose underlying
A\otimes R
-module is
M\oplus N
and whose
\tau
-linear morphism is
\tau_{M}\oplus\tau_{N}
.

-

Their
tensor product
, denoted
\underline{M}\otimes\underline{N}
, is defined to be
(M\otimes_{A\otimes R}N,\tau_{M}\otimes\tau_{N})
. The tensor operation admits
\mathbbm{1}
as a neutral object.

-

The
internal hom
is the object
\underline{\mathscr{H}}=\underline{\mathscr{H}\!\operatorname{om}}(\underline{M},\underline{N})
of
\mathscr{M}_{R}
representing the functor
\underline{X}\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{X}\otimes\underline{M},\underline{N})
. One verifies that its underlying module is
\mathscr{H}=\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(M,N)
with
\tau_{\mathscr{H}}
given by

\tau_{\mathscr{H}}:(\tau^{*}\mathscr{H})[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]=\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}((\tau^{*}M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}],(\tau^{*}N)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}],N[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}])=\mathscr{H}[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}],

mapping
h\mapsto\tau_{N}\circ h\circ\tau_{M}^{-1}
.

-

The
dual
of
\underline{M}
is defined to be the
A
-motive
\underline{\mathscr{H}\!\operatorname{om}}(\underline{M},\mathbbm{1})
.

-

Given
S
an
R
-algebra, there is a
base-change
functor
\mathscr{M}_{R}\to\mathscr{M}_{S}
mapping the
A
-motive
{\underline{M}=(M,\tau_{M})}
over
R
to
\underline{M}_{S}:=(M\otimes_{R}S,\tau_{M}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{id}_{S})
.

-

In the situation where
S
is a finitely presented flat
R
-module, there is also a
restriction functor

\operatorname{Res}_{S/R}:\mathscr{M}_{S}\to\mathscr{M}_{R}
mapping an
A
-motive
\underline{M}
over
S
to
\underline{M}
seen as an
A
-motive over
R
. Given two
A
-motives
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
over
R
and
S
respectively, we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{M},\operatorname{Res}_{S/R}\underline{N})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}_{S}}(\underline{M}_{S},\underline{N}).

In other words, the base-change functor is left-adjoint to the restriction functor.

Example 2.6

(Carlitz’s motive)
.

Let
C=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
be the projective line over
\mathbb{F}
and let
\infty
be the closed point of coordinates
[0:1]
. If
t
is any element in
\pazocal{O}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}}\setminus\{\infty\})
whose order of vanishing at
\infty
is
1
, we have an identification
A=\mathbb{F}[t]
. For an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra
R
, the tensor product
A\otimes R
is identified with
R[t]
. The morphism
\tau
acts on
p(t)\in R[t]
by raising its coefficients to the
q
th-power. It is rather common to denote by
p(t)^{(1)}
the polynomial
\tau(p(t))
. Let
\kappa:A\to R
be an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra morphism and let
\theta=\kappa(t)
. The ideal
\mathfrak{j}\subset R[t]
is principal, generated by
(t-\theta)
.

The Carlitz
\mathbb{F}[t]
-motive
\underline{C}
over
R
is defined by the couple
(R[t],\tau_{C})
where
\tau_{C}
maps
\tau^{*}p(t)
to
(t-\theta)p(t)^{(1)}
. Its
n
th tensor power
\underline{C}^{n}:=\underline{C}^{\otimes n}
is isomorphic to the
\mathbb{F}[t]
-motive whose underlying module is
R[t]
and where
\tau_{C^{n}}
maps
\tau^{*}p(t)
to
(t-\theta)^{n}p(t)^{(1)}
.

To match with number fields analogy, we let
\underline{A}(n):=\underline{C}^{-n}=(\underline{C}^{n})^{\vee}
. For
A=\mathbb{F}[t]
, this notation stresses that
\underline{A}(1)
plays the role of the number fields Tate motive
\mathbb{Z}(1)
and, more generally, that
\underline{A}(n)
plays the role of
\mathbb{Z}(n)
.

In particular, the category
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is additive and
A
-linear and it is a mere verification that that data of
(\mathscr{M}_{R},\otimes,\mathbbm{1})
forms a symmetric monoidal additive category which is rigid (
e.g.

[
HaJu
, §2.3]
).

In general, morphisms in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
might not admit kernel nor cokernel because the underlying module of the naturally defined kernel or cokernel might not be projective over
A\otimes R
. Nonetheless, there are some favorable situations where they are:

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
f:\underline{M}\to\underline{N}
be a morphism in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
.

1.

If its underlying map of modules is surjective, then
f
admits a kernel in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
and its underlying module is
\ker f
.

2.

If its underlying map of modules is a split injection, then
f
admits a cokernel in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
and its underlying module is
\operatorname{coker}f
.

Proof.

We only prove the first part, as the argument for the second one is similar. By assumption,
f:M\to N
is surjective. As
N
is projective,
f
admits a splitting, amounting to
M\cong\ker f\oplus N
. We deduce that
\ker f
is finite projective (as
M
is) and that the sequence
0\to(\tau^{*}\ker f)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\to(\tau^{*}M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\to(\tau^{*}N)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\to 0
is exact. By the universal property of
\ker f
, there exists a unique dashed arrow making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\tau^{*}\ker f)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\tau^{*}M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\tau^{*}N)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ker f)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\ker f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

and this dashed arrow is an isomorphism by the Snake Lemma. That is,
\underline{\ker}(f):=(\ker f,\tau_{\ker f})
defines an
A
-motive over
R
sitting in an sequence
\underline{S}:0\to\underline{\ker}(f)\to\underline{M}\to\underline{N}\to 0
.

It remains to check its universal property. Let
g:\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{M}
be a morphism in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
whose composition with
f
is zero. From the universal property of
\ker f
in
\mathbf{Mod}_{A\otimes R}
, there exists a unique
h:M^{\prime}\to\ker f
through which
g
factors. Since
\underline{S}
splits in
\mathbf{Mod}_{A\otimes R}
,
(\ker f)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
(resp.
(\tau^{*}\ker f)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}])
) is also a kernel of
f[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
(resp.
(\tau^{*}f)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
) and
g
factors uniquely through it. By uniqueness, we obtain
h[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]=\tau_{\ker f}\circ(\tau^{*}h)\circ\tau_{M^{\prime}}^{-1}[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
from which we deduce that
h
extends (uniquely) to a morphism
h:\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{\ker}(f)
.
∎

We end this paragraph by showing a useful corollary of Lemma
2.7
. Recall that a
retraction
in an additive category is a morphism
r:X\to Y
for which there exists a section
s:Y\to X
such that
r\circ s=\operatorname{id}_{Y}
; an additive category is called
weakly idempotent complete
if every retraction admits a kernel
[
Büh10
, §7]
. The following consequence will be important for the result in the appendix to apply, especially Proposition
A.5
.

Corollary 2.8
.

The additive category
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is weakly idempotent complete.

Proof.

A retraction in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is necessarily surjective at the level of modules, hence admits a kernel by
2.7
.
∎

Isogenies of
A
-motives

The category
\mathscr{M}_{R}
of
A
-motives over
R
is generally
not
abelian, even if
R=F
is a field. To remedy to the non-abelian feature, we shall introduce next the category
\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{R}
of
A
-motives
up to isogenies over
R
(see Definition
2.12
), which is abelian whenever
R=F
is a field. Following
[
Har3
, Def. 5.5, Prop. 5.8, Thm. 5.12, Cor. 5.15]
, we recall:

Proposition-Definition 2.9
.

A morphism
f:\underline{M}\to\underline{N}
in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is an
isogeny
if one of the following equivalent conditions is satisfied.

(a)

f
is injective and
\operatorname{coker}(f:M\to N)
is a finite projective
R
-module,

(b)

M
and
N
have the same rank and
\operatorname{coker}f
is finite over
R
,

(c)

there exists
0\neq a\in A
such that
f
induces an isomorphism of
(A\otimes R)[a^{-1}]
-modules
M[a^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}N[a^{-1}]
,

(d)

there exists
0\neq a\in A
and
g:\underline{N}\to\underline{M}
in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
such that
f\circ g=a\operatorname{id}_{N}
and
g\circ f=a\operatorname{id}_{M}
.

If an isogeny between
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
exists,
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
are said to be
isogenous
.

We then introduce the notion of
saturation
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
f:\underline{N}\to\underline{M}
be a morphism of
A
-motives.

1.

We call
f

saturated
, or say that
\underline{N}
is saturated in
\underline{M}
, if
f
admits a cokernel in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{Sat}(f)
denote the category of diagrams in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
g
is saturated, with morphisms in
\mathbf{Sat}(f)
being morphisms of diagrams which are the identity on both
\underline{N}
and
\underline{M}
. When it exists, we write an initial object in
\mathbf{Sat}(f)
–unique up to unique isomorphism–in the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{N}^{\operatorname{sat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\operatorname{sat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and call
f^{\operatorname{sat}}:\underline{N}^{\operatorname{sat}}\to\underline{M}
the
saturation of
f
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Assume that
R=F
is a field and let
f:\underline{N}\to\underline{M}
be a morphism in
\mathscr{M}_{F}
. Then
f
admits a kernel in
\mathscr{M}_{F}
, and
\mathbf{Sat}(f)
admits an initial object. If further
f
is a monomorphism, the canonical map
i:\underline{N}\to\underline{N}^{\operatorname{sat}}
is an isogeny.

Proof.

The choice of
R=F
a field makes
A\otimes F
into a Dedekind domain. Therefore, any torsion-free subquotient of a finite projective
A\otimes F
-module is itself finite projective; hence so is the module
\ker(f)
. That
\tau_{N}
induces an isomorphism
{\tau_{\ker f}:\tau^{*}\ker(f)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\ker(f)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}
and that
\underline{\ker}(f):=(\ker(f),\tau_{\ker f})
represents a kernel of
f
in
\mathscr{M}_{F}
is a mere verification similar to the proof of Lemma
2.7
.

For what remains, we assume–up to replacing
\underline{N}
by the quotient
\underline{N}/\underline{\ker}(f)
–that
f
is an inclusion of subobjects
\underline{N}\hookrightarrow\underline{M}
;
i.e.
on the underlying modules,
f
is the inclusion
N\subset M
. We consider the submodules

\displaystyle N^{\operatorname{sat}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{m\in M~|~\text{there~exists~}a\in A\otimes F:~a\cdot n\in N\right\},

\displaystyle(\tau^{*}N)^{\operatorname{sat}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{m\in\tau^{*}M~|~\text{there~exists~}a\in A\otimes F:~a\cdot n\in\tau^{*}N\right\}

of
M
and
\tau^{*}M
respectively. Both are finite projective
A\otimes F
-modules and so is
M/N^{\operatorname{sat}}
. One verifies that
\tau_{M}
induces an isomorphism

(\tau_{N})^{\operatorname{sat}}:(\tau^{*}N)^{\operatorname{sat}}[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}N^{\operatorname{sat}}[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}].

It remains to show that the couple
(N^{\operatorname{sat}},(\tau_{N})^{\operatorname{sat}})
forms an
A
-motive
\underline{N}^{\operatorname{sat}}
; namely, it suffices to prove the equality
\tau^{*}N^{\operatorname{sat}}=(\tau^{*}N)^{\operatorname{sat}}
as submodules of
\tau^{*}M
. That
\underline{N}^{\operatorname{sat}}\hookrightarrow\underline{M}
is a saturation of
f
will then be clear.

We consider the following ideal of
A\otimes F
:

\mathfrak{v}:=\operatorname{Ann}(N^{\operatorname{sat}}/N)=\{a\in A\otimes F~|~\text{for~all~}m\in N^{\operatorname{sat}}:~am\in N\}.

As the modules
(\tau^{*}N)^{\operatorname{sat}}/\tau^{*}N
and
N^{\operatorname{sat}}/N
are isomorphic away from
\mathfrak{j}
(via the induced map from
\tau_{M}
), we obtain

\mathfrak{v}=\operatorname{Ann}(N^{\operatorname{sat}}/N)=\operatorname{Ann}((\tau^{*}N)^{\operatorname{sat}}/\tau^{*}N))\subset\operatorname{Ann}(\tau^{*}N^{\operatorname{sat}}/\tau^{*}N)=\tau(\mathfrak{v})

as ideals of
(A\otimes F)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
, where we used
\tau^{*}N^{\operatorname{sat}}\subset(\tau^{*}N^{\operatorname{sat}})
for the above inclusion and
[
Stack
, Tag 07T8]
for the last equality. Using prime ideal decomposition in the Dedekind domain
A\otimes F
yields
\tau(\mathfrak{v})=\mathfrak{v}
. Consequently,
\mathfrak{v}
descends to an ideal of
A
from which we obtain
(\tau^{*}N)^{\operatorname{sat}}=\tau^{*}N^{\operatorname{sat}}
, as desired. In addition, this shows that the cokernel of
N\hookrightarrow N^{\operatorname{sat}}
is
A
-torsion, hence that
\underline{N}\to\underline{N}^{\operatorname{sat}}
is an isogeny (use definition

(
c
)

for
a\in A
a generator of a principal ideal in the form
\mathfrak{v}^{h}
).
∎

The previous lemma motivates the definition of the category of
A
-motives up to isogenies
(see
[
HaJu
, Def. 2.3.1]
).

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{R}
be the
K
-linear category whose objects are those of
\mathscr{M}_{R}
and where the hom-sets of two objects
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
is given by the
K
-vector space

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{R}}(\underline{M},\underline{N}):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{M},\underline{N})\otimes_{A}K.

We call the objects of
\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{R}
the
A
-motives over
R
up to isogenies
.

An isogeny in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
then becomes an isomorphism in
\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{R}
. In particular, over
R=F
a field, Lemma
2.11
implies that any morphism admits a kernel and a cokernel in
\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{F}
. The following is detailed in
[
HaJu
, Prop. 2.3.4]
:

Proposition 2.13
.

The category
\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{F}
is abelian.

2.2
Extension modules in
\mathscr{M}_{R}

In this subsection, we are concerned with the computation of extension modules in categories of
A
-motives. Theorem
A
of the introduction is proved below (Theorem
2.21
).

Let
R
be an
A
-algebra. As mentioned above, the category of
A
-motives over
R
is not abelian. However, there is a notion of exact sequences in the category
\mathscr{M}_{R}
which we borrow from
[
HaJu
, Rmk. 2.3.5(b)]
:

Definition 2.14
.

We say that a sequence
0\to\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{M}\to\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is
exact
if its underlying sequence of
A\otimes R
-modules is exact.

The next proposition appears and is discussed in
[
HaJu
, Rmk. 2.3.5(b)]
and will allow us to consider extension modules in Subsection
3.2
. Although stated in the case where
R
is a particular
A
-algebra and
\deg(\infty)=1
, its statement extends to our setting:

Proposition 2.15
.

The category
\mathscr{M}_{R}
together with the notion of exact sequences as in Definition
2.14
forms an exact category (Definition
A.1
).

Proof.

We consider the forgetful functor
F:\mathscr{M}_{R}\to\mathbf{Mod}_{A\otimes R}
. In particular, a sequence
\underline{S}
in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is exact if and only if
F(\underline{S})
is exact. By Lemma
2.7
, Proposition
A.8
applies to the functor
F
, thus completing the proof.
∎

By the above proposition, we can make sense of
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
but, as
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is not abelian, there is an ambiguity about what one means for
i>1
. This ambiguity is cleared up in the appendix where we defined higher extension modules in exact categories. By the fact that
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is weakly idempotent complete (Corollary
2.8
) and by the embedding Theorem
A.3

(

⁢
i
i

)

, higher extension modules in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
behave as well as in abelian categories.

Let
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
be two
A
-motives over
R
. The morphisms from
\underline{N}
to
\underline{M}
in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
are precisely the
A\otimes R
-linear maps of the underlying modules
f:N\to M
such that
\tau_{M}\circ\tau^{*}f=f\circ\tau_{N}
. The module of degree zero extensions is set to be the module of homomorphisms in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
, and hence:

\operatorname{Ext}^{0}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M}):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})=\{f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(N,M)~|~\tau_{M}\circ\tau^{*}f=f\circ\tau_{N}\}.

The next proposition computes the module of degree one extensions.

Proposition 2.16
.

There is a surjective morphism of
A
-modules, functorial in both
\underline{N}
and
\underline{M}
,

\iota:\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(\tau^{*}N,M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})

whose kernel is
\left\{f\circ\tau_{N}-\tau_{M}\circ\tau^{*}f~|~f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(N,M)\right\}
.
\iota
maps a morphism
u\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(\tau^{*}N,M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
to the class of the extension
[M\oplus N,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tau_{M}&u\\
0&\tau_{N}\end{smallmatrix}\right)]
in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})
.

Proof.

Consider an exact sequence
[\underline{E}]:0\to\underline{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\to}}\underline{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}\underline{N}\to 0
in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
, namely, an exact sequence of the underlying
A\otimes R
-modules with commuting
\tau
-action. Because
N
is a projective module, there exists
s:N\to E
a section of the underlying short exact sequence of
A\otimes R
-modules. We let
\xi:=i\oplus s:M\oplus N\to E
. This produces an equivalence of extensions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\oplus N,\xi^{-1}\circ\tau_{E}\circ\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

Because
\xi^{-1}\circ\tau_{M}\circ\xi
is an isomorphism from
\tau^{*}M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\oplus\tau^{*}N[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
to
M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\oplus N[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
which restricts to
\tau_{M}
on the left and to
\tau_{N}
on the right, there exists
u\in\operatorname{Hom}_{(A\otimes R)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}(\tau^{*}N[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}],M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}])=\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(\tau^{*}N,M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
such that
\xi^{-1}\circ\tau_{E}\circ\xi=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tau_{M}&u\\
0&\tau_{N}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. We have just shown that the map

\iota:\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(\tau^{*}N,M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M}),\quad u\longmapsto[M\oplus N,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tau_{M}&u\\
0&\tau_{N}\end{smallmatrix}\right)]

is onto. Note that
\iota(0)
corresponds to the class of the split extension. Further,
\iota(u+v)
corresponds to the Baer sum of
\iota(u)
and
\iota(v)
. In addition, given the exact sequence
[\underline{E}]
and
a\in A
, the pullback of multiplication by
a
on
N
by
\pi
gives another extension which defines
a\cdot[\underline{E}]
. If
[\underline{E}]=\iota(u)
, it is formal to check that
a\cdot[\underline{E}]=\iota(au)
. As such,
\iota
is a surjective
A
-module morphism. To find its kernel, it suffices to determine whenever
\iota(u)
is equivalent to the split extension. This happens if and only if there is a commutative diagram in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[M\oplus N,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tau_{M}&&u\\
0&&\tau_{N}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{N}}

where
h
is an isomorphism in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
. Since the diagram commutes in the category of
A\otimes R
-modules, it follows that
h
is of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\operatorname{id}_{M}&f\\
0&\operatorname{id}_{N}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for an
A\otimes R
-linear map
f:N\to M
. Because it is a diagram in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
, it further requires commuting
\tau
-action, that is:

\begin{pmatrix}\tau_{M}&u\\
0&\tau_{N}\end{pmatrix}\tau^{*}\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{id}_{M}&f\\
0&\operatorname{id}_{N}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{id}_{M}&f\\
0&\operatorname{id}_{N}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tau_{M}&0\\
0&\tau_{N}\end{pmatrix}.

The above equation amounts to
u=f\circ\tau_{N}-\tau_{M}\circ\tau^{*}f
, and hence

\ker(\iota)=\left\{f\circ\tau_{N}-\tau_{M}\circ\tau^{*}f~|~f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(N,M)\right\}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.17
.

Let
f:\underline{M}\to\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
be an epimorphism in
\mathscr{M}_{R}
. Then, the induced map
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M}^{\prime\prime})
is onto.

Proof.

Because
N
is finite projective over
A\otimes R
, so is
\tau^{*}N
and hence the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(\tau^{*}N,-)
is exact. That
f
is an epimorphism means that
f
is a surjective morphism of the underlying modules. The induced morphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(\tau^{*}N,M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(\tau^{*}N,M^{\prime\prime})[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]

is therefore surjective, and we conclude by Proposition
2.16
.
∎

As an consequence of the above corollary and Proposition
A.4
of the appendix, we record:

Proposition 2.18
.

The modules
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})
vanish for
i>1
.

Propositions
2.16
and
2.18
combine to show that the cohomology of the complex

\left[\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(N,M)\xrightarrow{\tau_{N}^{\vee}-\tau_{M}}\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes R}(\tau^{*}N,M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\right],

(2.1)

sitting in degree zero and one, computes the extension modules in the corresponding degree. In that respect, we make a slight abuse of notations in making the next definition:

Definition 2.19
.

We denote by
\operatorname{RHom}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})
the complex (
2.1
).

Remark 2.20
.

The notation ”
\operatorname{RHom}
” is generally reserved for the
derived
\operatorname{Hom}
, namely, the universal extension of the
\operatorname{Hom}
functor in the derived category. Here we do not consider a derived category, since
\mathscr{M}_{R}
is not abelian, and there is no confusion possible.

The category
(\mathscr{M}_{R},\otimes,\mathbbm{1})
is a monoidal exact category in the sense of Definition
A.9
. From Corollary
A.10
, we have functorial isomorphisms for all
i\geq 0
:

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M}\otimes\underline{N}^{\vee}).

(2.2)

In particular, there is no loss of generality in considering extension modules of the form
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
. From now on, we will be mainly interested in extension modules of the latter form. For readability, we introduce here and elsewhere the notation
(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M)
to designate the sub-
A
-module

(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M):=\left\{m-\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}m)~|~m\in M\right\}

of
M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
. We shall restate the main results of this section in this case (repeated from Theorem
A
of the introduction).

Theorem 2.21
.

The cohomology of
\operatorname{RHom}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
is computed by the cohomology of the complex of
A
-modules

\left[M\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M}}M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\right]

sitting in degree zero and one. Further, the natural
A
-linear surjection

\iota:M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M}),

whose kernel is
(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M)
, is given by mapping
m\in M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
to the class of the extension

0\to\underline{M}\to\left[M\oplus(A\otimes R),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tau_{M}&m\cdot\mathbf{1}\\
0&\mathbf{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\to\mathbbm{1}\to 0.

Remark 2.22
.

From
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{R}}(-,-)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}_{R}}(-,-)\otimes_{A}K
(
cf
. Definition
2.12
), the extension spaces of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{M}
in the
K
-linear category
\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{R}
are computed by the complex

\left[M\otimes_{A}K\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M}}M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]\otimes_{A}K\right].

2.3
Extensions having good reduction

We introduce here the function field analogue of the
\ell
-adic realization functor (Definition
2.24
), and show that it is exact (Proposition
2.27
). It will allow us to define
extensions with good reduction
next (Definition
2.30
).

Let
\ell
be a maximal ideal of
A
and denote by
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
the completed local ring of
A
at
\ell
. We let
F
be a field containing
K
and let
\kappa:A\to F
be the inclusion. Let
F^{s}
be a separable closure of
F
and denote by
G_{F}=\operatorname{Gal}(F^{s}|F)
the absolute Galois group of
F
equipped with the profinite topology. The group
G_{F}
acts
A
-linearly on the left-hand side of the tensor
A\otimes F^{s}
, and this action extends by continuity to an
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-linear action of
G_{F}
on the algebra

\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s}):=(A\otimes F^{s})^{\wedge}_{\ell}=\varprojlim_{n}~(A\otimes F^{s})/\ell^{n}(A\otimes F^{s})

leaving
\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F):=(A\otimes F)^{\wedge}_{\ell}
invariant. If
\mathbb{F}_{\ell}
denotes the residue field of
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
and
\pi
a uniformizer, we have an identification
\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F)=(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}\otimes F)[\![\pi]\!]
.

Remark 2.23
.

In the function field/number field dictionary, the assignment
R\mapsto\pazocal{A}(R)
is akin to the
p
-typical Witt vectors construction
R\mapsto W(R)
(
e.g.

[
Har1
, §1.1]
).

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\tau_{M})
be an
A
-motive over
F
of rank
r
. Let
\underline{M}_{F^{s}}=(M_{F^{s}},\tau_{M})
be the
A
-motive over
F^{s}
obtained from
\underline{M}
by base-change.
G_{F}
acts
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-linearly on:

(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}:=\varprojlim_{n}~(M\otimes_{F}F^{s})/\ell^{n}(M\otimes_{F}F^{s})=M\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})

and leaves the submodule
M^{\wedge}_{\ell}=M\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F)
invariant. Following
[
GAnd
, §1.8]
, we define:

Definition 2.24
.

The
\ell
-adic realization

\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
of
\underline{M}
consists of the
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-module

\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}:=\left\{m\in(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}~|~m=\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}m)\right\}

together with the compatible action of
G_{F}
which it inherits as a submodule of
(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
.

Remark 2.25
.

In
[
Mor2
]
, Mornev extended this construction to the situation where
\ell
is the closed point
\infty
.

The next lemma is well-known in the case of
\tau
-sheaves (
e.g.

[
TaWa
, Prop.6.1]
).

Lemma 2.26
.

The map
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}\otimes_{\pazocal{O}_{\ell}}\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})\to(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
,
\omega\otimes f\mapsto\omega\cdot f
is an isomorphism of
\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})
-modules. In particular, the
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-module
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
is free of rank
r
and the action of
G_{F}
on
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
is continuous.

Proof.

Let
n\geq 1
. In the ring
A\otimes F
, the ideals
\ell^{n}
and
\mathfrak{j}
are coprime. Hence, the following composition of
A\otimes F
-linear maps is a well-defined isomorphism:

\varphi_{n}:\tau^{*}(M/\ell^{n}M)\cong\frac{(\tau^{*}M)}{\ell^{n}(\tau^{*}M)}\cong\frac{(\tau^{*}M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}{\ell^{n}(\tau^{*}M)[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}{\ell^{n}M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}\cong M/\ell^{n}M.

The data of
\varphi_{n}
induces a semi-simple
q
-linear map
3
3

3

For
k
a field containing
\mathbb{F}
and
V
a
k
-vector space, an
\mathbb{F}
-linear endomorphism
f
of
V
is
q
-linear
if
f(rv)=r^{q}f(v)
for all
r\in k
and
v\in V
.
(in the sense of
[
Kat1
, §1]
) on the finite dimensional
F^{s}
-vector space:

(M\otimes_{F}F^{s})/\ell^{n}(M\otimes_{F}F^{s})=(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}/\ell^{n}(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}.

By Lang’s isogeny theorem (
e.g.

[
Kat1
, Prop. 1.1]
), the multiplication map

\{m\in(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}/\ell^{n}(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}~|~\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}m)=m\}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}F^{s}\to(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}/\ell^{n}(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}

(2.3)

is an isomorphism. Taking the inverse limit of (
2.3
) over all
n
yields the desired isomorphism.

As
(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
is free of rank
r
over
\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})
, the same is true for the module
(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}/\ell^{n}(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
over
(A/\ell^{n})\otimes F^{s}
. The isomorphism (
2.3
) implies that the
A/\ell^{n}
-module

[(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}/\ell^{n}(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}]^{\tau_{M}=1}:=\{m\in(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}~|~\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}m)=m\}

is free of rank
r
over
A/\ell^{n}
. Their projective limit
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
is thus a free
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-module of rank
r
.

By definition, the action of
G_{F}
on
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
is continuous if, and only if, for all
n
, the induced action of
G_{F}
on
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}/\ell^{n}\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
factors through
\operatorname{Gal}(E_{n}|F)
for some finite Galois extension
E_{n}
of
F
. Let
\textbf{t}=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}\}
be a basis of the finite dimensional
F
-vector space
M^{\wedge}_{\ell}/\ell^{n}M^{\wedge}_{\ell}
. Let
F_{M}
be the matrix of
\tau_{M}
written in the basis
\tau^{*}\textbf{t}
and
t
. Let
\bm{\omega}=\{\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{s}\}
be a basis of
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}/\ell^{n}\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
over
\mathbb{F}
. By (
2.3
),
\bm{\omega}
is a basis of
(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}/\ell^{n}(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
over
F^{s}
, and we let
w_{ij}\in F^{s}
be the coefficients of
\bm{\omega}
expressed in
\mathbf{t}
, that is, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
,
\omega_{i}=\sum{w_{ij}t_{j}}
. We let
E_{n}
denote the Galois closure of the finite separable extension
F(w_{ij}|(i,j)\in\{1,\ldots,s\}^{2})
of
F
in
F^{s}
. Then,

\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}/\ell^{n}\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}=\{m\in(M\otimes_{F}E_{n})/\ell^{n}(M\otimes_{F}E_{n})~|~\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}m)=m\}.

That is, the action of
G_{F}
factors through
\operatorname{Gal}(E_{n}|F)
, as desired.
∎

Proposition 2.27
.

The following sequence of
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}[G_{F}]
-modules is exact

0\to\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}\longrightarrow(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M}}(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}\longrightarrow 0.

Proof.

Everything is clear but the surjectivity of
\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M}
. From Lemma
2.26
, one reduces to the situation where
\underline{M}=\mathbbm{1}
is the unit
A
-motive, in which case
(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}=\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})=(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}\otimes F^{s})[\![\pi]\!]
and
\tau_{M}=\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{Frob}_{q}
. Let
f=\sum_{n\geq 0}{a_{n}\pi^{n}}
be a series in
(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}\otimes F^{s})[\![\pi]\!]
and let
b_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{\ell}\otimes F^{s}
be such that
[\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{F}_{\ell}}\otimes(\operatorname{id}-\operatorname{Frob}_{q})](b_{n})=a_{n}
(which exists as
F^{s}
is separably closed). Then,
g:=\sum_{n\geq 0}{b_{n}\pi^{n}}
in
\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})
satisfies

f=(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(g),

and hence
\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M}
is surjective.
∎

We obtain the following:

Corollary 2.28
.

The functor
\underline{M}\mapsto\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
, from
\mathscr{M}_{F}
to the category of continuous
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-linear
G_{F}
-representations, is exact.

Proof.

Let
S:0\to\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{M}\to\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an exact sequence in
\mathscr{M}_{F}
. The underlying sequence of
A\otimes F
-modules is exact, and because
\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})
is flat over
A\otimes F
(
e.g.

[
Bou
, AC.III §4, Thm. 3(iii)]
), the sequence of
\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})
-modules
(S_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
is exact. In particular, the next commutative diagram of
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-modules has exact rows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{\prime}_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{\prime\prime}_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{\prime}_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{\prime\prime}_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M^{\prime\prime}}}

and the Snake Lemma together with Proposition
2.27
yields that
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}S
is exact.
∎

Let
\underline{M}
be an
A
-motive over
F
. From Corollary
2.28
, the functor
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}
induces an
A
-linear morphism:

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M})

(2.4)

into the first continuous cohomology group of
G_{F}
with values in
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}
. The next proposition is devoted to the explicit determination of (
2.4
).

Proposition 2.29
.

There is a commutative diagram of
A
-modules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}{(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{M^{\wedge}_{\ell}}{(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M^{\wedge}_{\ell})}}
(
2.4
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

where the right vertical morphism maps the class of
f\in M^{\wedge}_{\ell}
to the class of the cocycle
\sigma\mapsto\xi-\,^{\sigma}\xi
,
\xi
being any solution in
(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
of
f=\xi-\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}\xi)
.

Proof.

We prove the commutativity. Let
[\underline{E}]:0\to\underline{M}\to\underline{E}\to\mathbbm{1}\to 0
be a class in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
of the form
\iota(m)
for some
m\in M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
(Theorem
2.21
). The
\ell
-adic realization
\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{E}
of
\underline{E}
is the
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}[G_{F}]
-module consisting of solutions
\xi\oplus a\in(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}\oplus\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})
of the equation

\begin{pmatrix}\tau_{M}&m\cdot\mathbf{1}\\
0&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tau^{*}\xi\\
\tau^{*}a\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
a\end{pmatrix}

(see Definition
2.24
). The above equality amounts to
a\in\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
and
\xi-\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}\xi)=am
. A splitting of
[\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{E}]
as a sequence of
\pazocal{O}_{\ell}
-modules corresponds to the choice of a particular solution
\xi_{m}\in(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
of
\xi-\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}\xi)=m
(whose existence is ensured by Proposition
2.27
). We then have

\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M}\oplus\pazocal{O}_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{E},\quad(\omega,a)\longmapsto(\omega+a\xi_{m},a).

It follows that the morphism (
2.4
) in the diagram in Proposition 2.29 maps
[\underline{E}]
to the class of the cocycle
(\sigma\mapsto\xi_{m}-{}^{\sigma}\xi_{m})
, where
\xi_{m}
is any solution in
(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
of the equation
\xi-\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}\xi)=m
. In other words, the square commutes.

It remains to check that the right vertical morphism is an isomorphism. Applying the functor of
G_{F}
-invariants to the short exact sequence of Proposition
2.27
, we obtain a long exact sequence of cohomology:

M^{\wedge}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M}}M^{\wedge}_{\ell}\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M})\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}).

Therefore, it is sufficient to prove that
\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell})
vanishes. We have
{(M_{F^{s}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}=M\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})}
,
G_{F}
acting on the right-hand side of the tensor. Hence, it is enough to prove that the module
\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s}))
vanishes. This follows from:

\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\varprojlim_{n}\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},(A/\ell^{n})\otimes F^{s})=\varprojlim_{n}(A/\ell^{n})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},F^{s})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}(0)

where we used
(1)
the explicit form
\pazocal{A}_{\ell}(F^{s})=(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}\otimes F^{s})[\![\pi]\!]
and
(2)
that
\operatorname{H}^{1}(G_{F},F^{s})
vanishes by the additive version of Hilbert’s 90 Theorem
[
Ser
, x.§1, Prop. 1]
.
∎

For the remainder of this section, let us assume that
F=F_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a local function field with valuation ring
\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}
. Let
F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\text{ur}}
be the maximal unramified extension of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
in
F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
. Let
I_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the inertia subgroup of
G_{\mathfrak{p}}=G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

Definition 2.30
.

Let
\underline{M}
be an
A
-motive over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and let
\ell
be a maximal ideal in
A
. We say that an extension
[\underline{E}]
of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{M}
has
good reduction with respect to
\ell
if
[\underline{E}]
lies in the kernel of
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})\to\operatorname{H}^{1}(I_{\mathfrak{p}},\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M})
. We denote by
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{good}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})_{\ell}
the kernel of (
2.4
), namely the module of extensions having good reduction with respect to
\ell
.

The proof of Proposition
2.29
with
I_{\mathfrak{p}}=\operatorname{Gal}(F^{s}_{\mathfrak{p}}|F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\text{ur}})
in place of
G_{F}
applies almost
verbatim
to show:

Proposition 2.31
.

There is a commutative diagram of
A
-modules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}^{1}(I_{\mathfrak{p}},\operatorname{T}_{\ell}\underline{M})}
