{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]}{(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{(M_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\text{ur}}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}}{(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})((M_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{ur}}})^{\wedge}_{\ell})}}
(
2.4
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

In particular, given
m\in M[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

The extension
\iota(m)
has good reduction with respect to
\ell
,

(b)

The equation
\xi-\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}\xi)=m
admits a solution
\xi
in
(M_{F^{\emph{ur}}_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
.

Definition
2.30
should presumably be independent of
\ell
. The analogous statement is the counterpart of Conjecture
(C

3

)
which we state next:

Conjecture 2.32

(Independence of
\ell
)
.

Assume
\kappa(A)\subset\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Let
[\underline{E}]
be an extension of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{M}
in the category
\mathscr{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
. If
[\underline{E}]
has good reduction with respect to an ideal
\ell
of
A
for which
\kappa(\ell)\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, then it has good reduction with respect to any ideal
\ell^{\prime}
with the same property.

3
Mixed
A
-motives and their extension modules

We discuss here the notion of mixedness for Anderson
A
-motives. In the case where
A
is a univariate polynomial algebra, the definition of
pure

t
-motives is traced back to the work of Anderson
[
GAnd
, 1.9]
.
Mixed

A
-motives were first mentioned in the talk of Pink at the Arbeitstagung in Bonn from 1997
4
4

4

Available at the address
https://people.math.ethz.ch/ pink/ftp/HS-AT97.pdf
.
. A systematic study, however, appeared only recently in the work of Hartl–Juschka
[
HaJu
, §3]
under the condition that the place
\infty
has degree one and the base field
R=F
is algebraically closed. Our presentation deals with the most general case: arbitrary curve
C
and place
\infty
, and over an arbitrary base field
F
. Compared to
[
Tae1
]
or
[
HaJu
]
, the new difficulty is to deal with non perfect fields
F
, since then, the slope filtration for isocrystals does not necessarily split.

3.1
Mixed Anderson
A
-motives

Isocrystals over a field

In this subsection, we present some materials on
function fields isocrystal
following
[
Mor2
]
. Our objective is to prove existence and uniqueness of the slope filtration with pure subquotients having increasing slopes. The general theory of slope filtrations has been developed in
[
YAnd
]
, and the results of interest for us on isocrystals appear in
[
Har2
]
in the case
A
is a univariate polynomial algebra. The new account of this subsection is the adaptation of
[
Har2
, Prop 1.5.10]
to allow more general ring
A
(see Theorem
3.10
). All of this are key steps towards the definition of weights and mixedness (Definition
3.20
).

We begin with some general notations. Let
R
be an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra and let
E
be a
local function fields
. By that, we mean that
E
is the field of Laurent series over a finite field extension
k
of
\mathbb{F}
in the formal variable
\pi
,
\pazocal{O}
the subring of
E
consisting of power series over
k
and
\mathfrak{m}
the maximal ideal of
\pazocal{O}
. Explicitly
E=k(\!(\pi)\!)
,
\pazocal{O}=k[\![\pi]\!]
and
\mathfrak{m}=\pi\pazocal{O}
. In the sequel,
E
will correspond to the local field of
(C,\pazocal{O}_{C})
at a closed point of
C
.

Extending the notation introduced in Subsection
2.3
in the context of the
\ell
-adic realization functor, we denote by
\pazocal{A}(R)
the completion of the ring
\pazocal{O}\otimes R
at its ideal
\mathfrak{m}\otimes R
:

\pazocal{A}(R):=\varprojlim_{n}~(\pazocal{O}\otimes R)/(\mathfrak{m}^{n}\otimes R)

(3.1)

and we let
\pazocal{B}(R)
be the ring
E\otimes_{\pazocal{O}}\pazocal{A}(R)
. Through the previous identifications, we readily check that
\pazocal{A}(R)=(k\otimes R)[\![\pi]\!]
and
\pazocal{B}(R)=(k\otimes R)(\!(\pi)\!)
. The invertible elements of
\pazocal{B}(R)
are described as:

\pazocal{B}(R)^{\times}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi^{n}\cdot\pazocal{A}(R)^{\times},\quad\pazocal{A}(R)^{\times}=(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}\otimes R)^{\times}+\pi\pazocal{A}(R).

Given
x\in\pazocal{B}(R)^{\times}
, we define
v_{\pi}(x)
as the unique
n\in\mathbb{Z}
for which
x\in\pi^{n}\cdot\pazocal{A}(R)^{\times}
.

Let
\tau:\pazocal{O}\otimes R\to\pazocal{O}\otimes R
, be the
\pazocal{O}
-linear map induced by
a\otimes r\mapsto a\otimes r^{q}
. We shall also denote by
\tau
its continuous extension to
\pazocal{A}(R)
or
\pazocal{B}(R)
. Observe that
v_{\pi}
is preserved by
\tau
. Again, we denote by
\mathbf{1}
the canonical
A\otimes R
-linear morphisms
\tau^{*}\pazocal{A}(R)\to\pazocal{A}(R)
and
\tau^{*}\pazocal{B}(R)\to\pazocal{B}(R)
.

For the remaining of this subsection, we assume that
R=F
is a field.

Definition 3.1
.

An
isocrystal
\underline{D}
over
F
is a pair
(D,\varphi_{D})
where
D
is a free
\pazocal{B}(F)
-module of finite rank and
\varphi_{D}:\tau^{*}D\to D
is a
\pazocal{B}(F)
-linear isomorphism.

A morphism
(D,\varphi_{D})\to(C,\varphi_{C})
of isocrystals is a
\pazocal{B}(F)
-linear morphism of the underlying modules
f:D\to C
such that
f\circ\varphi_{D}=\varphi_{C}\circ\tau^{*}f
. We let
\operatorname{IC}_{F}
be the category of isocrystals over
F
.

Remark 3.2
.

Pursuing the analogy of Remark
2.23
, isocrystals are the analogue of the eponymous object in
p
-adic Hodge theory (we refer to
[
Har1
, §3.5]
). In both settings, such objects carry a slope filtration (see Theorem
3.10
for the function fields one). For number fields, isocrystals are only defined at finite places, whereas for function fields, isocrystals are defined regardless of the finiteness of the place. In the next subsection, we use the slope filtration at
\infty
in order to define weights.

We define the
rank
\operatorname{rk}\underline{D}
of
\underline{D}
to be the rank of
D
over
\pazocal{B}(F)
. If
\underline{D}
is nonzero, let
b
be a basis of
D
and let
U
denote the matrix of
\varphi
expressed in
\tau^{*}\textbf{b}
and
b
. A different choice of basis
\textbf{b}^{\prime}
leads to a matrix
U^{\prime}
such that
U=\tau(P)U^{\prime}P^{-1}
for a certain invertible matrix
P
with coefficients in
\pazocal{B}(F)
. As such,
v_{\pi}(\det U)
–well-defined as
\det U
is invertible in
\pazocal{B}(R)
–is independent of
b
. We denote it by
\deg\underline{D}
and we name it the
degree of
\underline{D}
. We define the
slope of
\underline{D}
to be the rational number
\lambda(\underline{D})=\delta\deg\underline{D}/\operatorname{rk}\underline{D}
, where
\delta
is the degree of
k
over
\mathbb{F}
.

Remark 3.3
.

The normalizing factor
\delta
appearing in the slope was introduced by Mornev
[
Mor2
]
in order to manage consistently finite fields extensions.

From
[
Mor2
, Prop. 4.1.1]
, the category
\operatorname{IC}_{F}
is abelian. We can therefore consider exact sequences in
\operatorname{IC}_{F}
. The degree and rank are additive in short exact sequences, and the association
\underline{D}\mapsto-\lambda(\underline{D})
defines a slope function for
\operatorname{IC}_{F}
in the sense of
[
YAnd
, Def. 1.3.1]
. The second point of the next definition should be compared with
[
YAnd
, Def. 1.3.6]
:

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\underline{D}=(D,\varphi)
be an isocrystal over
F
.

1.

A
subisocrystal
of
\underline{D}
is an isocrystal
\underline{G}=(G,\varphi_{G})
for which
G\subset D
,
\varphi_{G}=\varphi_{D}|_{\tau^{*}G}
. The
quotient
of
\underline{D}
by
\underline{G}
is the pair
(D/G,\varphi_{D})
(this is indeed an isocrystal by
[
Mor2
, Prop. 4.1.1]
).

2.

The isocrystal
\underline{D}
is
semistable
(resp.
isoclinic
) if, for any nonzero subisocrystal
\underline{D}^{\prime}
of
\underline{D}
,
\lambda(\underline{D}^{\prime})\geq\lambda(\underline{D})
(resp.
\lambda(\underline{D}^{\prime})=\lambda(\underline{D})
).

Semistability and isoclinicity are related to the notion of
purity
, borrowed from
[
Mor2
, Def. 3.4.6]
, that we next recall. We first require the definition of
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattices
:

Definition 3.5
.

Let
D
be a free
\pazocal{B}(F)
-module of finite rank. An
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice in
D
is a sub-
\pazocal{A}(F)
-module of finite type of
D
which generates
D
over
\pazocal{B}(F)
.

Observe that any
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice
L
in
D
is free, and that its rank is the rank of
D
over
\pazocal{B}(F)
. We denote by
\langle\varphi_{D}L\rangle
the sub-
\pazocal{A}(F)
-module
\varphi_{D}(\tau^{*}L)
in
D
: it is again an
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice in
D
since
\varphi_{D}
is an isomorphism. We define
\langle\varphi_{D}^{n}L\rangle
inductively to be the
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice
\langle\varphi_{D}\langle\varphi_{D}^{n-1}L\rangle\rangle
. To include the
n=0
-case, we agree on
\langle\varphi_{D}^{0}L\rangle=L
.

Definition 3.6
.

A nonzero isocrystal
(D,\varphi_{D})
over
F
is said to be
pure of slope
\lambda
if there exist an
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice
L
in
D
and integers
s
and
r>0
such that
\langle\varphi_{D}^{r\delta}L\rangle=\mathfrak{m}^{s}L
and
\lambda=s/r
. By convention, the zero isocrystal is pure with
no slope
.

Example 3.7
.

Let
D
be the free
\pazocal{B}(F)
-module of rank
r\geq 1
with basis
\{e_{0},\ldots,e_{r-1}\}
and let
\varphi_{D}:\tau^{*}D\to D
be the unique linear map such that
\varphi_{D}(\tau^{*}e_{i-1})=e_{i}
for
{1\leq i<r}
and
\varphi_{D}(\tau^{*}e_{r-1})=\pi^{s}e_{0}
. Then
(D,\varphi_{D})
is a pure isocrystal of slope
\delta s/r
with
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice given by
\pazocal{A}(F)e_{0}\oplus\cdots\oplus\pazocal{A}(F)e_{s-1}
.

The following lemma relates the definition of slopes from purity and from slope functions. It also implies that one can refer to
the
slope of a pure isocrystal:

Lemma 3.8
.

If
\underline{D}
is a pure isocrystal of slope
\lambda
, then
\lambda(\underline{D}^{\prime})=\lambda
for any nonzero sub-isocrystal
\underline{D}^{\prime}
of
\underline{D}
. In particular,
\underline{D}
is isoclinic (hence semistable).

Proof.

Let
L
be an
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice in
D
such that
\langle\varphi^{r\delta}L\rangle=\mathfrak{m}^{s}L
for integers
r>0
and
d
such that
\lambda=s/r
(whose existence is ensured by the definition of pureness). If
\underline{D}^{\prime}=(D^{\prime},\varphi)
is a nonzero subisocrystal of
\underline{D}
, then
L^{\prime}=L\cap D^{\prime}
is an
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice in
D^{\prime}
such that
\langle\varphi^{r\delta}L^{\prime}\rangle=\mathfrak{m}^{s}L^{\prime}
. As
L^{\prime}
is nonzero, let
\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell}\}
be a basis of
L^{\prime}
over
\pazocal{A}(F)
. We have

(\det\varphi)^{r\delta}(t_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge t_{\ell})=\mathfrak{m}^{s\ell}(t_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge t_{\ell})\quad\text{in}~\bigwedge^{\ell}L^{\prime}.

Hence
r\delta\deg\underline{D}^{\prime}=s\operatorname{rk}\underline{D}^{\prime}
, which yields

\lambda(\underline{D}^{\prime})=\frac{\delta\deg\underline{D}^{\prime}}{\operatorname{rk}\underline{D}^{\prime}}=\frac{s}{r}=\lambda.

∎

Definition 3.9
.

A
slope filtration for
\underline{D}
is an increasing sequence of sub-isocrystals of
\underline{D}

0=\underline{D}_{0}\subsetneq\underline{D}_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\underline{D}_{s}=\underline{D},

satisfying:

(i)

for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
,
\underline{D}_{i}/\underline{D}_{i-1}
is semistable,

(ii)

we have
\lambda(\underline{D}_{1})<\lambda(\underline{D}_{2}/\underline{D}_{1})<\cdots<\lambda(\underline{D}_{s}/\underline{D}_{s-1})
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
\underline{D}
be an isocrystal over
F
. Then
\underline{D}
carries a unique slope filtration:

0=\underline{D}_{0}\subsetneq\underline{D}_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\underline{D}_{s}=\underline{D}.

(3.2)

In addition, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
, the quotients
\underline{D}_{i}/\underline{D}_{i-1}
are pure isocrystals. In particular, the conditions pure, semi-stable and isoclinic are equivalent.

Remark 3.11
.

The proof presented below relies on
[
Har2
, Prop. 1.5.10]
which already uses Dieudonné-Manin classification (in the case
A=\mathbb{F}[t]
). It would be much more satisfactory to prove the equivalence between semistability and isoclinicity directly, so that Theorem
3.10
would follow from André’s theory.

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

The existence and uniqueness of the slope filtration follows from
[
YAnd
, Thm 1.4.7]
applied to the slope function
\underline{D}\mapsto-\lambda(\underline{D})
on the abelian category
\operatorname{IC}_{F}
.

Hence, we only need to prove existence of (
3.2
) with pure subquotients since uniqueness follows from
[
YAnd
, Thm 1.4.7]
. If
\delta=1
, then
\pazocal{A}(F)
is identified with
F[\![\pi]\!]
and Theorem
3.10
is proved in
[
Har2
, Prop. 1.5.10]
. We now explain how the general case reduces to this one. Let
\mathbb{G}
be the finite field extension of
\mathbb{F}
corresponding to

\mathbb{G}:=\{f\in\bar{\mathbb{F}}\cap F~|~f^{q^{\delta}}=f\}.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{G}\to F
denote the inclusion. This defines an embedding of
\mathbb{G}
in
k
, the residue field of
E
. Let
\pazocal{A}_{\phi}(F)
be the completion of
\pazocal{O}\otimes_{\mathbb{G}}F
at the ideal
\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{G}}F
. In the theory of isocrystals over
F
with
\mathbb{G}
in place of
\mathbb{F}
,
\pazocal{A}_{\phi}(F)
appears in place of
\pazocal{A}(F)
and
\delta=1
. In
[
Mor2
, §4.2]
, Mornev defines an additive functor

[\phi]^{*}:\left(\pazocal{A}(F)-\text{isocrystals}\right)\longrightarrow\left(\pazocal{A}_{\phi}(F)-\text{isocrystals}\right)

which, by
[
Mor2
, Prop 4.2.2]
(see also
[
BoHa
, Prop 8.5]
), is an equivalence of categories such that
[\phi]^{*}(\underline{D})
is a pure isocrystals of slope
\lambda
if
\underline{D}
is. Let

[\phi]_{*}:\left(\pazocal{A}_{\phi}(F)-\text{isocrystals}\right)\longrightarrow\left(\pazocal{A}(F)-\text{isocrystals}\right)

be a quasi-inverse of
[\phi]^{*}
and let
\ell:[\phi]_{*}[\phi]^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{id}
be a natural transformation.

Let
\underline{D}
be an
\pazocal{A}(F)
-isocrystal. We now prove existence of (
3.2
) with pure subquotients for
\underline{D}
. By
[
Har2
, Prop. 1.5.10]
, there exists an increasing sequence of sub-
\pazocal{A}_{\phi}(F)
-isocrystals of
[\phi]^{*}\underline{D}
:

0=\underline{G}_{0}\subsetneq\underline{G}_{1}\subsetneq\underline{G}_{2}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\underline{G}_{s}=[\phi]^{*}\underline{D}

the subquotients
\underline{G}_{i}/\underline{G}_{i-1}
being pure of slopes
\lambda_{i}
with
\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{s}
. Applying
[\phi]_{*}
and then
\ell
, we obtain

0=\underline{D}_{0}\subsetneq\underline{D}_{1}\subsetneq\underline{D}_{2}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\underline{D}_{s}=\underline{D}

(3.3)

with
\underline{D}_{i}:=\ell([\phi]_{*}[\phi]^{*}\underline{D}_{i})
for all
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,s\}
. We claim that the isocrystals
\underline{D}_{i}/\underline{D}_{i-1}
are pure of slope
\lambda_{i}
. Indeed, we have

\underline{D}_{i}/\underline{D}_{i-1}\cong[\phi]_{*}\underline{G}_{i}/[\phi]_{*}\underline{G}_{i-1}\cong[\phi]_{*}(\underline{G}_{i}/\underline{G}_{i-1})

where the last isomorphism comes from the fact that
[\phi]_{*}
is an exact functor (any equivalence of categories is exact). Because
\underline{G}_{i}/\underline{G}_{i-1}
is pure of slope
\lambda_{i}
,
\underline{D}_{i}/\underline{D}_{i-1}
is also pure of slope
\lambda_{i}
. We conclude that (
3.3
) is the slope filtration for
\underline{D}
and satisfies the assumption of the theorem.
∎

Let
\underline{D}
be an isocrystal over
F
. It is useful to rewrite the slope filtration of
\underline{D}
as
(\underline{D}_{\lambda_{i}})_{1\leq i\leq s}
for rational numbers
\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{s}
, where the successive quotients
\underline{D}_{\lambda_{i}}/\underline{D}_{\lambda_{i-1}}
are pure of slope
\lambda_{i}
. We let
D_{\lambda_{i}}
be the underlying module of
\underline{D}_{\lambda_{i}}
. For
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
, let
\underline{D}_{\lambda}
be the subisocrystal of
\underline{D}
whose underlying module is

D_{\lambda}:=\bigcup_{\lambda_{i}\leq\lambda}D_{\lambda_{i}}.

We also let

\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}:=\underline{D}_{\lambda}/\bigcup_{\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda}\underline{D}_{\lambda^{\prime}},

the symbol
\cup
being understood as the isocrystal whose underlying module is given by the union.

Corollary 3.12
.

For all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
, the assignment
\operatorname{IC}_{F}\to\operatorname{IC}_{F}
,
\underline{D}\mapsto\underline{D}_{\lambda}
defines an exact functor. Equivalently, any morphism
f:\underline{D}\to\underline{C}
of isocrystals over
F
is
strict
with respect to the slope filtration, that is:

\text{for~all~}\lambda\in\mathbb{Q},\quad f(D_{\lambda})=f(D)\cap C_{\lambda}.

Proof.

This follows at once from Theorem
3.10
and Lemma
3.8
that any semistable isocrystal is isoclinic. Hence, the corollary follows from
[
YAnd
, Thm 1.5.9]
.
∎

We observe that the slope filtration is not split in general. However it does when the ground field
F
is perfect:

Theorem 3.13
.

If
F
is perfect, the slope filtration of
\underline{D}
splits,
i.e.

\underline{D}
decomposes along a direct sum

\underline{D}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the argument given for Theorem
3.10
: the corresponding result for
\delta=1
is proven in
[
Har2
, Prop 1.5.10]
and the general
\delta
-case is easily deduced from
[
Mor2
, Prop 4.2.2]
.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

The above theorem is the
Dieudonné-Manin decomposition for isocrystals
. When
F
is algebraically closed, given
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
there exists a unique (up to isomorphisms) simple and pure isocrystal
\underline{S}_{\lambda}
of slope
\lambda
(see
[
Mor2
, Prop 4.3.4]
). Any pure isocrystal of slope
\lambda
decomposes as a direct sum of
\underline{S}_{\lambda}
(see
[
Mor2
, Prop 4.3.7]
) and together with Theorem
3.13
yields the
Dieudonné-Manin classification
(see
[
Lau
]
). It does not hold for any
F
, even separably closed, as noticed by Mornev in
[
Mor2
, Rmk 4.3.5]
(see also Example
3.26
).

Isocrystals attached to
A
-motives

We now explain how to attach isocrystals to
A
-motives over fields. This construction (Definition
3.16
) will be required next in the definition of
the weights
of
A
-motives (Definition
3.20
).

Let
R
be an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra and let
\kappa:A\to R
be an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra morphism. We choose the rings
\pazocal{A}(R)
and
\pazocal{B}(R)
of the previous paragraph in the following way. Given a closed point
x
on
C
, we let
O_{x}\subset K
be the associated discrete valuation ring with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
. We denote by
\pazocal{O}_{x}
the completion of
O_{x}
and by
K_{x}
the completion of
K
. We let
\mathbb{F}_{x}
denote the residue field of
x
. We let
\pazocal{A}_{x}(R)
and
\pazocal{B}_{x}(R)
be the rings obtained by completing
\pazocal{O}_{x}\otimes R
and
K_{x}\otimes R
for the
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
-adic topology (as in (
3.1
)).

Recall that
\mathfrak{j}_{\kappa}
is the ideal of
A\otimes R
generated by
\{a\otimes 1-1\otimes\kappa(a)~|~a\in A\}
.

Lemma 3.15
.

We have
\mathfrak{j}_{\kappa}\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(R)=\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(R)
. For
x
a closed point of
C
distinct from
\infty
such that
\kappa(\mathfrak{m}_{x})R=R
, we further have
\mathfrak{j}_{\kappa}\pazocal{A}_{x}(R)=\pazocal{A}_{x}(R)
.

Proof.

We prove the first assertion. Let
a
be a non constant element of
A
so that
a^{-1}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
. Then
a\otimes 1-1\otimes\kappa(a)\in\mathfrak{j}
is invertible with
-\sum_{n\geq 0}{a^{-(n+1)}\otimes\kappa(a)^{n}}
as inverse, where the infinite sum converges in
\pazocal{A}_{\infty}(R)\subset\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(R)
.

We prove the second assertion. From
\kappa(\mathfrak{m}_{x})R=R
, there exists a relation of the form
1=\sum_{i}{\kappa(\ell_{i})r_{i}}
for some
\ell_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}_{x}
and
r_{i}\in R
. Then, the element

\sum_{i}{(1\otimes\kappa(\ell_{i})-\ell_{i}\otimes 1)\cdot(1\otimes r_{i})}

lies in
1+(\mathfrak{m}_{x}\otimes R)\subset\pazocal{A}_{x}(R)^{\times}
. It also belongs to
\mathfrak{j}_{\kappa}
, and hence
\mathfrak{j}_{\kappa}\pazocal{A}_{x}(R)=\pazocal{A}_{x}(R)
.
∎

In order to use the results of the previous paragraph, we now assume that
R=F
is a field and that
x
is a closed point of
C
distinct from
\ker\kappa
. Let
\underline{M}=(M,\tau_{M})
be an
A
-motive over
F
.

Lemma
3.15
yields an isomorphism of
\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
-modules:

\tau_{M}\otimes_{A\otimes F}\mathbf{1}:\tau^{*}(M\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{B}_{x}(F))\xrightarrow{\sim}M\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{B}_{x}(F).

This motivates the following construction:

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\operatorname{I}_{x}(M)
be the
\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
-module
M\otimes_{A\otimes R}\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
. We define
\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{M})
as the pair
(\operatorname{I}_{x}(M),\tau_{M}\otimes\mathbf{1})
.

Because of the next proposition, we may call the datum of
\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{M})
the
isocrystal attached to
\underline{M}
.

Proposition 3.17
.

We have the following:

(i)

\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{M})
is an isocrystal over
F
.

(ii)

If
x\neq\infty
,
\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{M})
is pure of slope
0
.

Proof.

Because
M
is locally free of constant rank and
\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
is a finite product of fields,
\operatorname{I}_{x}(M)
is a free
\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
-module. Thus, point

(
i
)

follows from Lemma
3.15
. To prove

(

⁢
i
i

)

, it suffices to note that
L=M\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{A}_{x}(F)
is an
\pazocal{A}_{x}(F)
-lattice in
M\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
such that
\langle\tau_{M}L\rangle=L
.
∎

Proposition 3.18
.

The assignment
\operatorname{I}_{x}:\underline{M}\mapsto\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{M})
defines an exact functor from the category of
A
-motives to the category of isocrystals at
x
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
is flat over
A\otimes F
. First note that
\pazocal{O}_{x}\otimes F
is Noetherian, so that its completion
\pazocal{A}_{x}(F)
for the
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
-adic topology is flat over it. Tensoring by
K_{x}
over
\pazocal{O}_{x}
, we obtain that
\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
is flat over
K_{x}\otimes F
. Yet the latter is flat over
A\otimes F
, which concludes.
∎

The next lemma clarifies the relation between the isocrystal of an
A
-motive and that of its saturation (Definition
2.10
and Lemma
2.11
). It will be important in the next subsection:

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
f:\underline{N}\to\underline{M}
be an isogeny. Then
\operatorname{I}_{x}(f):\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{N})\to\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{M})
is an isomorphism. In particular, given
\underline{P}
a sub-
A
-motive of
\underline{M}
and
\underline{P}^{\operatorname{sat}}
its saturation in
\underline{M}
,
\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{P})=\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{P}^{\operatorname{sat}})
. If
\underline{Q}
is another sub-
A
-motive of
\underline{M}
such that
\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{P})=\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{Q})
inside
\operatorname{I}_{x}(\underline{M})
, then
\underline{P}^{\operatorname{sat}}=\underline{Q}^{\operatorname{sat}}
.

Proof.

The cokernel of an isogeny is
A
-torsion hence is annihilated under tensoring with
\pazocal{B}_{x}(F)
, and the assertions of the second and third sentence of the lemma follow.

We prove the statement in the last sentence. Because
\operatorname{I}_{x}
is exact, the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\cap Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

is transformed to a pullback square, and it follows that
\operatorname{I}_{x}(P\cap Q)=\operatorname{I}_{x}(P)=\operatorname{I}_{x}(Q)
. In particular,
P\cap Q
,
P
and
Q
are locally free of the same rank. By the definition of saturation (Definition
2.10
)
P^{\operatorname{sat}}/(P\cap Q)^{\operatorname{sat}}
is an
A
-motive over
F
. By the hypotheses it maps to zero under
\operatorname{I}_{x}
. Because
\operatorname{I}_{x}
is rank preserving, this quotient is zero, and by symmetry in
P
and
Q
we deduce
P^{\operatorname{sat}}=(P\cap Q)^{\operatorname{sat}}=Q^{\operatorname{sat}}
.
∎

Weight filtration and mixedness

As before, let
F
be a field containing
\mathbb{F}
, let
\kappa:A\to F
be an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra morphism and let
\underline{M}
be an
A
-motive over
F
. Let
\underline{D}:=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})
be the isocrystal at the place
\infty
attached to
\underline{M}
. By Theorem
3.10
,
\underline{D}
carries a unique slope filtration:

0=\underline{D}_{0}\subsetneq\underline{D}_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\underline{D}_{s}=\underline{D}

(3.4)

with ascending slopes
\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{s}
, where
\lambda_{i}:=\lambda(\underline{D}_{i}/\underline{D}_{i-1})
.

Definition 3.20
.

We call the set
w(\underline{M}):=\{-\lambda_{i}~|~1\leq i\leq s\}
the
set of weights
of
\underline{M}
.

The next definition dates back to the seminal paper of Anderson
[
GAnd
, 1.9]
.

Definition 3.21
.

We call
\underline{M}

pure of weight
\mu
if
\underline{D}
is pure of slope
-\mu
. Equivalently,
\underline{M}
is pure of weight
\mu
if
w(\underline{M})=\{\mu\}
.

The sign convention–weights opposed to slopes–is made to fit with the number field situation:

Example 3.22
.

Using notations of Example
2.6
, the Carlitz twist
\underline{A}(1)
over
F
is pure of weight
-1
and, more generally,
\underline{A}(n)
is pure of weight
-n
. It is analogous to the number field case, where the motive
\mathbb{Z}(n)
is pure of weight
-2n
(the factor
2
, reflecting the degree
[\mathbb{C}:\mathbb{R}]
, could be removed by renormalizing the weight filtration using half-integral integers).

In analogy with number fields, we define mixedness for
A
-motives as follows:

Definition 3.23
.

We call
\underline{M}

mixed
if there exist rational numbers
\mu_{1}<\ldots<\mu_{s}
and an increasing finite filtration by saturated sub-
A
-motives of
\underline{M}
:

0=W_{\mu_{0}}\underline{M}\subsetneq W_{\mu_{1}}\underline{M}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq W_{\mu_{s}}\underline{M}=\underline{M},

(3.5)

for which the successive quotients
W_{\mu_{i}}\underline{M}/W_{\mu_{i-1}}\underline{M}
are pure of weight
\mu_{i}
.

Before pursuing on the properties of mixedness, let us explain why being mixed is a very restrictive condition over imperfect base fields. Suppose that
\underline{M}
is a mixed
A
-motive and consider a filtration as in (
3.5
). The functor
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}
is exact (Proposition
3.18
), and applying it to (
3.5
) yields a finite filtration of
\underline{D}=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})
by subisocrystals:

0=\underline{D}^{0}\subsetneq\underline{D}^{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\underline{D}^{s}=\underline{D}

(3.6)

whose successive quotients
\underline{D}^{i}/\underline{D}^{i-1}
are pure isocrystals of slope
-\mu_{i}
. Note that the slopes of this filtration are decreasing, hence (
3.6
) is
not
the slope filtration of
\underline{D}
.

Proposition 3.24
.

If
\underline{M}
is mixed, the slope filtration of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})
splits.

To prove
3.24
, we require a lemma on isocrystals:

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
S:0\to\underline{D}\to\underline{D}^{\prime}\to\underline{D}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an exact sequence of isocrystals, where
\underline{D}
and
\underline{D}^{\prime\prime}
are pure and
\lambda(\underline{D})>\lambda(\underline{D}^{\prime\prime})
. Then
S
is split.

Proof.

Because
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{IC}_{F}}(\underline{D}^{\prime\prime},\underline{D})\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{IC}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{D}\otimes(\underline{D}^{\prime\prime})^{\vee})
by Corollary
A.10
, the argument reduces to the case of
\underline{D}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbbm{1}
and
\lambda(\underline{D})>0
(
e.g.

[
Mor2
, Prop. 3.4.10]
). Following the proof of Proposition
2.16
, one sees that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{IC}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{D})
is isomorphic to
D/\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{id}-\varphi)
as a
K
-vector space, where by
\operatorname{id}-\varphi
we mean the
K
-linear endomorphism of
D
given by
d\mapsto d-\varphi(\tau^{*}d)
. In particular, to show that any sequence
S
splits, it is enough to show that
\operatorname{id}–\varphi
is surjective on
D
. As
\underline{D}
is pure of positive slope,
D
contains an
\pazocal{A}(F)
-lattice
L
satisfying
\langle\varphi^{h}L\rangle\subset\mathfrak{m}L
for some
h>0
. This implies that the series

\xi_{d}:=d+\varphi(\tau^{*}d)+\varphi(\tau^{*}\varphi(\tau^{*}d))+\ldots

converges in
D
for all
d\in D
, and the assignment
d\mapsto\xi_{d}
defines an inverse of
\operatorname{id}-\varphi
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.24
.

Let
\underline{D}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\underline{D}^{\prime}_{s}
be pure isocrystals and let
\underline{D}^{\prime\prime}
be a pure isocrystal such that
\lambda(\underline{D}^{\prime\prime})>\lambda(\underline{D}^{\prime}_{i})
for all
i
. Then, by Lemma
3.25
,

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{IC}_{F}}\left(\underline{D}^{\prime\prime},\bigoplus_{i}\underline{D}_{i}^{\prime}\right)\cong\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\operatorname{IC}_{F}}\left(\underline{D}^{\prime\prime},\underline{D}_{i}^{\prime}\right)=(0).

Using the above observation, by induction one shows that
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(W_{\mu_{i}})
splits for all
i
, and for
i=s
we obtain a splitting of the filtration (
3.6
). By reordering the pure parts, we build another split filtration of
\underline{D}
:

0\subsetneq\underline{D}^{s}/\underline{D}^{s-1}\subsetneq(\underline{D}^{s}/\underline{D}^{s-1})\oplus(\underline{D}^{s-1}/\underline{D}^{s-2})\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\underline{D}^{i}/\underline{D}^{i-1}

having increasing slopes. By uniqueness, it coincides with the slope filtration of
\underline{D}
.
∎

Proposition
3.24
allows one to construct non mixed
A
-motives when
F
is imperfect, by constructing an
A
-motive whose associated isocrystal is non split. One observes that by Theorem
3.13
such examples can only exist if
F
is imperfect. Example
3.26
below show that such examples exist.

Example 3.26
.

We suppose that
A=\mathbb{F}[t]
and denote by
\pi
the uniformizer
t^{-1}
in
\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(F)
, then identified with
F(\!(\pi)\!)
. Assume that
F
is imperfect, and let
\alpha\in F
be such that
-\alpha
does not have any
q
th root in
F
. Consider the
t
-motive
\underline{M}
whose underlying module is
F[t]e_{0}\oplus F[t]e_{1}
, and where
\tau_{M}
acts by
\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}e_{0})=e_{0}
and
\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}e_{1})=\alpha e_{0}+(t-\theta)^{-1}e_{1}
.
\underline{M}
inserts in a short exact sequence of
t
-motives:

0\to\mathbbm{1}\cdot e_{0}\to\underline{M}\to\underline{A}(1)\cdot e_{1}\to 0.

(3.7)

We prove that the slope filtration of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})
does not split. More precisely, let us show that
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\mathbbm{1})\cdot e_{0}
is the only non-zero strict subisocrystal of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})
.

Let
\underline{L}\neq 0
be a strict subisocrystal of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})
.
\underline{L}
must have rank
1
over
F(\!(\pi)\!)
, and we let
\ell_{0}e_{0}+\ell_{1}e_{1}
be a generator of its underlying module. Let
f\in F(\!(\pi)\!)
be a non zero element such that
\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}(\ell_{0}e_{0}+\ell_{1}e_{1}))=f(\ell_{0}e_{0}+\ell_{1}e_{1})
. This yields the equation:

\begin{pmatrix}1&\alpha\\
0&\pi(1-\theta\pi)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tau(\ell_{0})\\
\tau(\ell_{1})\end{pmatrix}=f\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{0}\\
\ell_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

Because
\underline{L}\neq 0
, the first row implies
\ell_{0}\neq 0
. If
\ell_{1}\neq 0
, as
(1-\theta\pi)
is a unit in
F[\![\pi]\!]
the bottom row imposes
v_{\pi}(f)=1
, whereas the first row reads

\tau(\ell_{0})+\alpha\tau(\ell_{1})=f\ell_{0}.

(3.8)

Because
v_{\pi}(f)=1
, (
3.8
) also implies that
v_{\pi}(\ell_{0})=v_{\pi}(\ell_{1})
. In particular, if
l_{0}
and
l_{1}
are the first nonzero coefficients in
F
of
\ell_{0}
and
\ell_{1}
in
F(\!(\pi)\!)
respectively, (
3.8
) gives

\alpha=-(l_{0}/l_{1})^{q}.

This is in contradiction with our assumption. Hence
\ell_{1}=0
and
f=\tau(\ell_{0})/\ell_{0}
. Therefore,
\underline{L}
coincides with
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\mathbbm{1})\cdot e_{0}
.

In this example,
\underline{M}
is an extension of
\underline{A}(1)
by
\mathbbm{1}
, two
t
-motives of respective weights
-1<0
. When the weights goes in ascending orders, such examples do not appear anymore (see Proposition
3.37
).

Remark 3.27
.

Observe that the converse of Proposition
3.24
does not hold: there are non-mixed
A
-motives over perfect base fields (
e.g.

[
HaJu
, Ex. 2.3.13]
). For a partial converse, we refer to Proposition
3.30
below.

Proposition-Definition 3.28
.

If
\underline{M}
is mixed, a filtration
W=(W_{\mu_{i}}\underline{M})_{1\leq i\leq s}
as in (
3.5
) is unique. In addition, the set
\{\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s}\}
equals
w(\underline{M})
.

(i)

For all
i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
, we let
W_{\mu_{i}}M
be the underlying module of
W_{\mu_{i}}\underline{M}
.

(ii)

For all
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
, we set

\displaystyle W_{\mu}M

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{\mu_{i}\leq\mu}{W_{\mu_{i}}M},\quad W_{\mu}\underline{M}:=(W_{\mu}M,\tau_{M}),

\displaystyle W_{<\mu}M

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{\mu_{i}<\mu}{W_{\mu_{i}}M},\quad W_{<\mu}\underline{M}:=(W_{<\mu}M,\tau_{M}),

and
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mu}\underline{M}:=W_{\mu}\underline{M}/W_{<\mu}\underline{M}
. Both
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mu}\underline{M}
and
W_{\mu}\underline{M}
, as well as
W_{<\mu}\underline{M}
, define mixed
A
-motives over
F
for all
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
.

(iii)

We call
(W_{\mu}\underline{M})_{\mu\in\mathbb{Q}}
the
weight filtration of
\underline{M}
.

(iv)

We let
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
(resp.
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{F}
) be the subcategory of
\mathscr{M}_{F}
(resp.
\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{F}
) whose objects are mixed, and whose morphisms
f:\underline{M}\to\underline{N}
preserves the weight filtration:

\text{for~all~}\mu\in\mathbb{Q}:\quad f(W_{\mu}M)\subset W_{\mu}N.

Remark 3.29
.

We shall prove below (Corollary
3.31
) that every morphism of
A
-motives
f:\underline{M}\to\underline{N}
,
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
being mixed, preserves the weight filtration. In other words, that
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
is a full subcategory of
\mathscr{M}_{F}
.

Proof of Proposition
3.28
.

Let
\underline{D}=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})
. By Proposition
3.24
, we have a canonical decomposition:

\underline{D}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda_{i}}\underline{D},

(3.9)

\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda_{i}}\underline{D}
being canonically identified with a pure subisocrystal of
\underline{D}
of slope
\lambda_{i}
. On the other-hand, let
W
be as in (
3.5
). By uniqueness of the decomposition (
3.9
), we have equalities of subisocrystals of
\underline{D}
:

\forall\mu\in\mathbb{Q}:\quad\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(W_{\mu}\underline{M})=\bigoplus_{\lambda_{i}\geq-\mu}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda_{i}}\underline{D}.

(3.10)

We conclude by Lemma
3.19
that the above identity determines
W
uniquely. The fact that
w(\underline{M})=\{\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s}\}
also follows.
∎

Next, we suggest a criterion for mixedness:

Proposition 3.30
.

Suppose that the slope filtration of
\underline{D}
splits. For all
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
, let
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})^{\mu}
be the subisocrystal of
\underline{D}
corresponding to
\bigoplus_{\lambda_{i}\geq-\mu}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda_{i}}\underline{D}
. Denote by
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}
its underlying module. Then, we have:

\text{for~all~}\mu\in\mathbb{Q}:\quad\operatorname{rank}_{A\otimes F}(\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap M)\leq\operatorname{rank}_{\pazocal{B}(F)}\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})^{\mu}

with equality if and only if
\underline{M}
is mixed. In the latter case,
W_{\mu}M=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap M
.

Proof.

First note that, for all
\mu
, the couple
\underline{M}_{\mu}:=(\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap M,\tau_{M})
defines a saturated sub-
A
-motive of
\underline{M}
. Furthermore, since the underlying module of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M}_{\mu})
corresponds to the completion of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap(M\otimes_{A}K)
for the
\infty
-adic topology,
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M}_{\mu})
defines a subisocrystal of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})^{\mu}
. The rank being additive in short exact sequences in the category of isocrystals, we get the desired inequality:

\operatorname{rank}_{A\otimes F}(\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap M)=\operatorname{rank}\underline{M}_{\mu}=\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M}_{\mu})\leq\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})^{\mu}.

If this is an equality for all
\mu
, then we obtain
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M}_{\mu})=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})^{\mu}
and deduce that the family
(\underline{M}_{\mu})_{\mu}
satisfies the requested property of Definition
3.23
.

Conversely, if
\underline{M}
is mixed, the following sequence of inclusions holds:

(W_{\mu}M)\otimes_{A}K\subset\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap(M\otimes_{A}K)\subset\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}.

Note that the left-hand side forms a dense subset of the right-hand side for the
\infty
-adic topology. Hence, the first inclusion is an inclusion of a dense subset. Taking the completion, we obtain
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(W_{\mu}M)=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap M)=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{M})^{\mu}
. The corresponding ranks are thus equal.

To conclude, as both
W_{\mu}M
and
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)_{\mu}\cap M
are saturated submodules of
M
, we deduce the equality
W_{\mu}M=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap M
from Lemma
3.19
.
∎

As an important corollary of the above formula for
W
, we obtain:

Corollary 3.31
.

Let
f:\underline{M}\to\underline{N}
be a morphism of
A
-motives,
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
being mixed. Then,
f
preserves the weight filtration:

\text{for~all~}\mu\in\mathbb{Q}:\quad f(W_{\mu}M)\subset W_{\mu}N.

In particular, for all
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
, the assignment
\underline{M}\mapsto W_{\mu}\underline{M}
defines a functor from the category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
to itself.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
. The claim follows from the following two easy observations:

1.

If
\underline{M}
is mixed, then so is
W_{\mu}\underline{M}
,

2.

If
f:\underline{M}\to\underline{N}
is a morphism of mixed
A
-motives, then the associated morphism of isocrystals maps
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}
to
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(N)^{\mu}
, and by Proposition
3.30
we have
{f(W_{\mu}M)\subset W_{\mu}N}
.

∎

Remark 3.32
.

We end this subsection by describing how weights behave under linear algebra type operations. Proofs are presented in
[
HaJu
, Prop. 2.3.11]
and extend without change to our larger setting. First note that
\mathbbm{1}
is a pure
A
-motive over
F
of weight
0
. Given two mixed
A
-motives
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
, their biproduct
\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N}
is again mixed with weight filtration
W_{\mu}(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N})=W_{\mu}\underline{M}\oplus W_{\mu}\underline{N}
(
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
). Their tensor product
\underline{M}\otimes\underline{N}
is also mixed, with
\lambda
-part of its weight filtration being:

W_{\lambda}(\underline{M}\otimes\underline{N})=\left(\sum_{\mu+\nu=\lambda}{W_{\mu}\underline{M}\otimes W_{\nu}\underline{N}}\right)^{\text{sat}}.

We took the saturation
A
-motive to ensure that the above is a saturated sub-
A
-motive of
\underline{M}\otimes\underline{N}
. The dual
\underline{M}^{\vee}
is mixed, and the
\mu
-part of its weight filtration
W_{\mu}\underline{M}
has for underlying module
W_{\mu}M^{\vee}=\{m\in M^{\vee}|\forall\lambda<-\mu:~m(W_{\lambda}M)=0\}^{\text{sat}}
. In general, given
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
two
A
-motives over
F
(without regarding whether
\underline{M}
or
\underline{N}
are mixed) and an exact sequence
0\to\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{M}\to\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
in
\mathscr{M}_{F}
, we have

w(\underline{0})

=

\emptyset

w(\underline{M}^{\vee})

=

-w(\underline{M})

w(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N})

=

w(\underline{M})\cup w(\underline{N})

w(\underline{M})

=

w(\underline{M}^{\prime})\cup w(\underline{M}^{\prime\prime})

w(\underline{M}\otimes\underline{N})

=

\{w+v~|~w\in w(\underline{M}),~v\in w(\underline{N})\}

3.2
Extension modules of mixed
A
-motives

As before, let
F
be a field containing
\mathbb{F}
and consider an
\mathbb{F}
-algebra morphism
\kappa:A\to F
. In this subsection, we are concerned with extension modules in the category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
. The next proposition shows that they are well-defined.

Proposition 3.33
.

The category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
together with the notion of exact sequences of Definition
2.14
is an exact category.

To prove Proposition
3.33
, we use Proposition
A.8
of the appendix applied once again to the forgetful functor
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}\to\mathbf{Mod}_{A\otimes R}
as in proof of Proposition
2.15
. Checking the required hypothesis of Proposition
A.8
amounts to showing:

Proposition 3.34
.

Let
0\to\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{M}\to\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an exact sequence of
A
-motives over
F
. If
\underline{M}
is mixed and one of
\underline{M}^{\prime}
or
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
is mixed, then all three are mixed.

Before proving (
3.34
), we begin by an intermediate result on isocrystals:

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
0\to\underline{D}^{\prime}\to\underline{D}\to\underline{D}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an exact sequence of isocrystals over
F
. If the slope filtration of
\underline{D}
and one of
\underline{D}^{\prime}
or
\underline{D}^{\prime\prime}
split, then all three are split.

Proof.

Assume that the slope filtration of
\underline{D}
and
\underline{D}^{\prime}
are split. Then
\underline{D}
decomposes as a finite direct sum of pure subisocrystals
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}
. We have a commutative diagram of subisocrystals of
\underline{D}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{D}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}}(\underline{D}^{\prime}\cap\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(3.11)

where the symbol
\cap
is understood as the subisocrystal whose underlying module is given by the intersection. We claim that
\iota
is an isomorphism. Firstly, it is injective as one checks by a diagram chase. By assumption,
\underline{D}^{\prime}
also decomposes as a direct sum of subisocrystals
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}^{\prime}
which, when non zero, are pure of slope
\lambda
. By functoriality of the slope filtration, the inclusion
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\underline{D}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\underline{D}
factors through
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}
. Hence,

\operatorname{rk}\underline{D}^{\prime}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}}{\operatorname{rk}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}}{\operatorname{rk}(\underline{D}^{\prime}\cap\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D})}\leq\operatorname{rk}\underline{D}^{\prime}

where the last inequality follows from the injectivity of
\iota
. All the above inequalities are thus equalities, from which we deduce that
\iota
is an isomorphism.

Therefore, we can extend the square (
3.11
) into exact sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}}(\underline{D}^{\prime}\cap\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D}/(\underline{D}^{\prime}\cap\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\underline{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{D}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{D}^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

where the dashed arrow exists and is unique by the cokernel property of the upper right term. By the Snake Lemma, it is an isomorphism. In particular,
\underline{D}^{\prime\prime}
decomposes as a direct sum of pure subisocrystals, and by uniqueness, we deduce that its slope filtration splits.

The argument for the second part of the statement is similar enough to the first one to be skipped.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.34
.

Suppose
\underline{M}
and
\underline{M}^{\prime}
(resp.
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
) are mixed. For
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
, let
W_{\mu}M^{\prime}:=W_{\mu}M\cap M^{\prime}
and
W_{\mu}M^{\prime\prime}:=f(W_{\mu}M)^{\text{sat}}
, where
f
is the epimorphism
\underline{M}\twoheadrightarrow\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
. They are canonically endowed with
A
-motive structures denoted by
W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime}
and
W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
respectively, and our task is to prove that
(W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime})_{\mu}
and
(W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime})_{\mu}
satisfy the property of Definition
3.5
.

By Lemma
3.35
, the slope filtrations of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{N})
for
\underline{N}\in\{\underline{M},\underline{M}^{\prime},\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\}
are split, and, for
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
, we denote by
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{N})^{\mu}
the direct sum of the subisocrystals of
\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(\underline{N})
which are pure of slope
\geq-\mu
.

Since
\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(F)
is flat over
A\otimes F
, the functor
-\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(F)
commutes with finite intersections (
e.g.

[
Bou
, §I.2, Prop. 6]
):

\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(W_{\mu}M^{\prime})=(W_{\mu}M\cap M^{\prime})\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(F)=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu}\cap\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M^{\prime})=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M^{\prime})^{\mu}

where the last equality follows from the fact that the category of isocrystals has strict morphisms for the slope filtration (Corollary
3.12
). Similarly,

\displaystyle\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(W_{\mu}M^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=f(W_{\mu}M)^{\text{sat}}\otimes_{A\otimes F}\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(F)=f(\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M)^{\mu})=\operatorname{I}_{\infty}(M^{\prime\prime})^{\mu}.

This shows that
\underline{M}^{\prime}
and
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
are both mixed with respective weight filtrations
(W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime})_{\mu\in\mathbb{Q}}
and
(W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime})_{\mu\in\mathbb{Q}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.36
.

For all
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
, the functor
\underline{M}\mapsto W_{\mu}\underline{M}
is exact on
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}^{\operatorname{iso}}
.

Proof.

Let
0\to\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an exact sequence of mixed
A
-motives. By the proof of Proposition
3.34
, we have
W_{\mu}M^{\prime}=W_{\mu}M\cap M^{\prime}
. Hence, the sequence
0\to W_{\mu}M^{\prime}\to W_{\mu}M\to W_{\mu}M^{\prime\prime}\to 0
is left-exact. We also have
W_{\mu}M^{\prime\prime}=f(W_{\mu}\underline{M})^{\text{sat}}
, so that the
A
-motive
\underline{\operatorname{im}}(f|W_{\mu}\underline{M})
is isogeneous to
W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
. In particular, the sequence
0\to W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime}\to W_{\mu}\underline{M}\to W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
is exact in
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}^{\text{iso}}
.
∎

According to Proposition
3.33
, we can consider extension modules of two mixed
A
-motives in
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
. By Corollary
3.31
, we have an equality

\operatorname{Ext}^{0}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M},\underline{N})=\operatorname{Ext}^{0}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M},\underline{N}),

but this does not necessary hold for higher extension modules.
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M},\underline{N})
can be interpreted as a submodule of
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M},\underline{N})
, but in general
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}
is not even a submodule of
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}
(
i>1
).

Proposition 3.37
.

Let
0\to\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an exact sequence of
A
-motives in
\mathscr{M}_{F}
. If
\underline{M}^{\prime}
and
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
are mixed, and if the smallest weight of
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
is bigger than the largest weight of
\underline{M}^{\prime}
, then
\underline{M}
is mixed.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.16
, there exists
u\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A\otimes F}(\tau^{*}M^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime})[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
and a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{\prime}\oplus M^{\prime\prime},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tau_{M^{\prime}}&&u\\
0&&\tau_{M^{\prime\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

which commutes in
\mathscr{M}_{F}
. From the the weight assumption on
\underline{M}^{\prime}
and
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
,
u
automatically respects the weight filtration: for all
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
,
u(\tau^{*}W_{\mu}M^{\prime\prime})\subset W_{\mu}M^{\prime}
. In particular,

\underline{M}_{\mu}:=\xi\left(W_{\mu}M^{\prime}\oplus W_{\mu}M^{\prime\prime},\begin{pmatrix}\tau_{M^{\prime}}&u\\
0&\tau_{M^{\prime\prime}}\end{pmatrix}\right)

defines a sub-
A
-motive of
\underline{M}
inserting in a short exact sequence in
\mathscr{M}_{F}
:

0\longrightarrow W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\underline{M}_{\mu}\longrightarrow W_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow 0.

(3.12)

Similarly,
\underline{M}_{<\mu}:=\xi\left(W_{<\mu}M^{\prime}\oplus W_{<\mu}M^{\prime\prime},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tau_{M^{\prime}}&u\\
0&\tau_{M^{\prime\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)
defines a sub-
A
-motive of
\underline{M}_{\mu}
. By (
3.12
), we obtain an exact sequence of
A
-motives:

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\underline{M}_{\mu}/\underline{M}_{<\mu}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{\mu}\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow 0.

(3.13)

The extremal terms of (
3.13
) are pure of weight
\mu
and at least one of them is zero,
and thus the middle term is pure of weight
\mu
. We deduce that the increasing sequence
(\underline{M}_{\mu})_{\mu}
of sub-
A
-motives of
\underline{M}
satisfies the condition of Definition
3.23
. Therefore
\underline{M}
is mixed.
∎

Remark 3.38
.

Contrary to the number fields situation, the full subcategory of
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}
consisting of pure
A
-motives over
F
is not semi-simple. This follows easily from the equality
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{N},\underline{M})
for two pure motives of the same weight.

Proposition
3.37
implies that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M}^{\prime\prime},\underline{M}^{\prime})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M}^{\prime\prime},\underline{M}^{\prime})
whenever the weights of
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
are bigger than the biggest weight of
\underline{M}^{\prime}
. In general this is not true. In this direction we record:

Proposition 3.39
.

Let
0\to\underline{M}^{\prime}\to\underline{M}\to\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an exact sequence of
A
-motives where
\underline{M}^{\prime}
and
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
are mixed. We assume that all the weights of
\underline{M}^{\prime\prime}
are strictly smaller than the smallest weight of
\underline{M}^{\prime}
. Then, the sequence is torsion in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M}^{\prime\prime},\underline{M}^{\prime})
if, and only if,
\underline{M}
is mixed.

Proof of Proposition
3.39
.

By Corollary
A.10
of the appendix, taking
\underline{N}:=\underline{M}^{\prime}\otimes(\underline{M}^{\prime\prime})^{\vee}
we can assume that the exact sequence is of the form
S:~0\to\underline{N}\to\underline{E}\to\mathbbm{1}\to 0
, the mixed
A
-motive
\underline{N}
having positive weights. In view of Theorem
2.21
, we may assume that
\underline{E}=\iota(u)
for some
u\in N[\mathfrak{j}^{-1}]
. Note that
0
is a weight of
\underline{E}
, the smallest.

If
\underline{E}
is mixed, then
\underline{E}
contains a sub-
A
-motive
\underline{L}=(L,\tau_{N})
of weight
0
which is isomorphic to
\mathbbm{1}
. Let
(m\oplus a)\in N\oplus(A\otimes F)
be a generator of
L
over
A\otimes F
. We have

\begin{pmatrix}\tau_{N}&u\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tau^{*}m\\
\tau^{*}a\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}m\\
a\end{pmatrix}.

This amounts to
a\in A
and
au\in\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{N})
, and then that
a[\underline{E}]=0
in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{N})
. Conversely, if there exists a nonzero
a\in A
such that
a[\underline{E}]
is split, Theorem
2.21
implies that there exists
m\in N
such that
au=m-\tau_{N}(\tau^{*}m)
. The nonzero
A\otimes F
-module
L
generated by
m\oplus a
together with
\tau_{N}
defines a sub-
A
-module of
\underline{E}
isomorphic to
\mathbbm{1}
. For all
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
, we define
A\otimes F
-modules

E_{\mu}:=W_{\mu}N+\mathbf{1}_{\mu\geq 0}L,\quad E_{<\mu}:=W_{<\mu}N+\mathbf{1}_{\mu>0}L

where
(W_{\mu}\underline{N})_{\mu\in\mathbb{Q}}
is the weight filtration of
\underline{N}
, and where
\mathbf{1}_{\mu\in S}
is the indicator function of the set
S
. It is easy to see that
\underline{E}_{\mu}:=(E_{\mu},\tau_{N})
and
\underline{E}_{<\mu}:=(E_{<\mu},\tau_{N})
define sub-
A
-motives of
\underline{E}
such that

\underline{E}_{\mu}/\underline{E}_{<\mu}\cong\begin{cases}0~&\text{ if }~\mu<0,\\
\underline{L}~&\text{ if }~\mu=0,\\
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mu}\underline{N}~&\text{ if }~\mu>0.\end{cases}

As desired, we have constructed an increasing sequence
(\underline{E}_{\mu})_{\mu}
of sub-
A
-motives of
\underline{E}
satisfying the property of Definition
3.23
. Hence,
\underline{E}
is mixed.
∎

Remark 3.40
.

Under the same hypothesis, Proposition
3.39
can be rephrased into

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M}^{\prime\prime},\underline{M}^{\prime})^{\text{tors}}=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\underline{M}^{\prime\prime},\underline{M}^{\prime}).

In particular, the
K
-vector space
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}^{\text{iso}}_{F}}(\underline{M}^{\prime\prime},\underline{M}^{\prime})
vanishes. The latter is only conjectured to be true in the number fields setting (
[
Del
, §1.3]
).

Remark 3.41
.

Nevertheless,
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
is generally non zero for
\underline{M}
having positive weights. In the notations of Example
2.6
, let
n
be a positive integer that is a power of the characteristic
p
, and consider the
A
-motive
\underline{A}(-n)
over a field extension
F
of
K
that contains a
(q-1)
-st root
\eta
of
(-1/\theta)^{n}
. We claim that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{A}(-n))
is non zero, equivalently that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{A}(-n))
has non zero torsion. Indeed, let
[\underline{E}]:=\iota(\eta^{q})
. Then,

-t^{n}\cdot[\underline{E}]=\iota(-t^{n}\eta^{q})=\iota(\eta-(t-\theta)^{n}\eta^{q})=0.

On the other-hand,
[\underline{E}]
is non zero: for degree reasons, there does not exist
p(t)\in F[t]
such that
\eta^{q}=p(t)-(t-\theta)^{n}p(t)^{(1)}
.

Theorem 3.42
.

Let
\underline{M}
be a mixed
A
-motive over
F
. For
i>1
, the
A
-module
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
is torsion.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be the
K
-linear abelian category
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}^{\text{iso}}
. From the relation

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}^{\text{iso}}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{F}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}K,

it suffices to show that the left-hand side vanishes for
i>1
. Let us first treat the case where
\underline{M}
only has non positive weights. Let
\mathscr{A}_{0}
be the full subcategory of
\mathscr{A}
consisting of objects whose weights are all non positive. As
\underline{M}
is an object of
\mathscr{A}_{0}
, note that

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{M}_{F}^{\text{iso}}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})

where the last equality follows from Proposition
3.37
. From Corollary
2.17
, we deduce that the functor
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}(\mathbbm{1},-)
is right-exact on
\mathscr{A}_{0}
hence
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=(0)
(Proposition
A.4
). Now, from the exactness of
W_{0}
over
\mathscr{A}
(Corollary
3.36
), any
e\in\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
produces a commutative diagram in
\mathscr{A}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{0}e:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{0}\underline{E}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{0}\underline{E}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

from which we deduce
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})
as desired. Now, let
\underline{M}
have arbitrary weights. Applying
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},-)
to the exact sequence:

0\longrightarrow W_{0}\underline{M}\longrightarrow\underline{M}\longrightarrow\underline{M}/W_{0}\underline{M}\longrightarrow 0,

we obtain from Proposition
3.39
that the natural map:

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},W_{0}\underline{M})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})

is surjective. Given an epimorphism
f:\underline{M}\to\underline{N}
in
\mathscr{A}
, we obtain a commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},W_{0}\underline{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},W_{0}\underline{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{N}).}

The left vertical arrow is surjective: indeed,
W_{0}f:W_{0}\underline{M}\to W_{0}\underline{N}
is an epimorphism by Corollary
3.36
, and we already proved that over
\mathscr{A}_{0}
the functor
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},-)=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}(\mathbbm{1},-)
is right-exact. Hence, the right vertical arrow is surjective and the functor
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},-)
is right-exact on
\mathscr{A}
. Hence
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})=(0)
(Proposition
A.4
again).
∎

4
Models and the integral part of
A
-motivic cohomology

In this section we illustrate the notion of
maximal integral models
. For
A
-motives, maximal integral models are understood as an analogue of Néron models of abelian varieties. The notion dates back to Gardeyn’s work on
models of
\tau
-sheaves

[
Gar2
]
and their reduction
[
Gar1
]
, where he proved a Néron-Ogg-Shafarevič type criterion (see Proposition
4.49
in our context). However our setting differs by the fact that, in opposition to
\tau
-sheaves,
A
-motives might not be
effective
. We also removed Gardeyn’s assumption for an
integral model
to be locally free. We will show in Theorems
4.32
and
4.53
that this is automatic for maximal ones over local and global function fields. Our presentation thus avoids the use of a technical lemma due to Lafforgue in Gardeyn’s exposition
[
Gar2
, §2]
. In that sense, the content of this section is original.

In practice, to make maximal integral models of
A
-motives explicit is a difficult task. In Subsection
4.1
, we consider the easier problem of finding maximal integral models of
Frobenius spaces
. Those are pairs
(V,\varphi)
where
V
is a finite dimensional vector space over a local field
E
containing
\mathbb{F}
and
\varphi
is a
q
-linear endomorphism of
V
. We show in Proposition
4.2
that there exists a unique
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattice in
V
stable by
\varphi
and which is maximal for this property. We end this section by the review of Katz’s equivalence of categories, and its application to study maximal integral models.

In Subsections
4.2
and
4.3
, we shall be concerned with integral models of
A
-motives. Given an inclusion
R\subset S
of
A
-algebras and an
A
-motive
\underline{M}=(M,\tau_{M})
over
S
, an
R
-model for
\underline{M}
is a finite sub-
A\otimes R
-module of
M

stable by
\tau_{M}
(Definition
4.19
).

We study the case where
\underline{M}
is an
A
-motive over a local function field
S=E
and where
R=\pazocal{O}_{E}
is its valuation ring in Subsection
4.2
. In Proposition
4.30
, we prove existence and uniqueness of integral
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-models which are maximal for the inclusion, and we prove that they are projective in Theorem
4.32
. We show that, given a well-chosen maximal ideal
\ell\subset A
and a positive integer
n
, the data of
(M/\ell^{n}M,\tau_{M})
defines a Frobenius space over
E
. Theorem
4.41
, our main result of this section, describes how to recover the maximal integral model of
\underline{M}
in terms of the data of the maximal integral model of
(M/\ell^{n}M,\tau_{M})
for all
n
. The latter is of fundamental importance in the proof of Theorem
D
, and permits to obtain the Néron-Ogg-Shafarevič type criterion for
A
-motives (Proposition
4.49
).

In Subsection
4.3
, we treat the case where
\underline{M}
is an
A
-motive over a global function field
S=F
where
R
is a Dedekind domain whose fraction field is
F
. If
\mathfrak{p}
is a non zero prime ideal of
R
, we obtain an
A
-motive
\underline{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
by base field extension from
F
to its completion
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Our Proposition
4.51
explains how to recover the maximal integral model of
\underline{M}
from the data of the maximal integral models of
\underline{M}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
for all
\mathfrak{p}
.

The full force of this section is used in Subsection
4.4
to prove Theorems
D
and
E
of the introduction (respectively Theorems
4.60
and
4.62
in the text).

4.1
Models of Frobenius spaces

In this subsection we work with notations that are slightly more general to what we need in the sequel. Let
(\pazocal{O}_{E},v_{E})
be a discrete valuation ring that contains the field
\mathbb{F}
. We denote by
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}_{E}
its maximal ideal and we fix
\varpi
a uniformizer. We let
E
be the field of fractions of
\pazocal{O}_{E}
. For what we have in mind, it will be enough to take for
\pazocal{O}_{E}
a valuation subring of a function field over
\mathbb{F}
or its completion. We let
\sigma:E\to E
denote the
q
-Frobenius on
E
(it fixes
\mathbb{F}
).

Our object of study are pairs
(V,\varphi)
where
V
is a finite dimensional
E
-vector space and
\varphi:\sigma^{*}V\to V
is an
E
-linear isomorphism. In the existing literature, they are generally referred to as
étale finite
\mathbb{F}
-shtukas over
E
(
e.g.

[
Har3
, §4]
). We prefer here the shorter name
Frobenius spaces
. By an
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattice in
V
we mean a finitely generated sub-
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module
L
of
V
which generates
V
over
E
. A sub-
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module
L
is stable by
\varphi
if
\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)\subset L
.

Definition 4.1
.

We say that
L
is an
integral model for
(V,\varphi)
if
L
is an
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattice in
V
stable by
\varphi
. We say that
L
is
maximal
if it is not strictly included in another integral model for
(V,\varphi)
.

Proposition 4.2
.

A maximal integral model for
(V,\varphi)
exists and is unique.

Proof.

Our proof follows closely
[
Gar2
, Prop. 2.2, 2.13]
. First note that there exists an integral model. Indeed, let
T^{\prime}
be an arbitrary
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattice in
V
. There exists a positive integer
k
such that
\varphi(\sigma^{*}T^{\prime})\subset\varpi^{-k}T^{\prime}
. We let
T:=\varpi^{k}T^{\prime}
so that

\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)=\varpi^{qk}\varphi(\sigma^{*}T^{\prime})\subset\varpi^{(q-1)k}T^{\prime}=\varpi^{(q-2)k}T\subset T.

Hence, the
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module
T
is an
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattice in
V
stable by
\varphi
.

We turn to the existence and uniqueness of the maximal integral model. If
L^{\prime}\subset L
is an inclusion of integral models, we have:

\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)/\varphi(\sigma^{*}L^{\prime})\right)=q\cdot\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(L/L^{\prime}\right).

We define the
discriminant of
L
to be the non-negative integer

\Delta(L):=\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(L/\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)\right).

Since,

\displaystyle\Delta(L^{\prime})-\Delta(L)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(L^{\prime}/\varphi(\sigma^{*}L^{\prime})\right)-\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(L/\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)/\varphi(\sigma^{*}L^{\prime})\right)-\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(L/L^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=(q-1)\cdot\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(L^{\prime}/L\right),

(4.1)

we have
\Delta(L^{\prime})>\Delta(L)
whenever the inclusion
L^{\prime}\subset L
is strict.

Now let
L
be an integral model with minimal discriminant. We claim that
L
equals the union of all integral models of
(V,\varphi)
, which proves both existence and uniqueness of the maximal integral model. Indeed, if
L^{\prime}
is another integral model for
(V,\varphi)
not contained in
L
, then
L+L^{\prime}
is an integral model such that the inclusion
L\subset L+L^{\prime}
is strict. But this contradicts the minimality assumption as we would have
\Delta(L)>\Delta(L+L^{\prime})
.
∎

Example 4.3
.

Assume that we are in the local function field situation, namely
\pazocal{O}_{E}=k[\![\varpi]\!]
for some field
k
containing
\mathbb{F}
. Suppose
V:=E
,
f\in\pazocal{O}_{E}
a nonzero element and
\varphi
is the morphism corresponding to
x\mapsto fx^{q}
. Write
f=u\varpi^{k}h^{q-1}
where
u\in\pazocal{O}_{E}^{\times}
,
0\leq k<q-1
is an integer and
h\in\pazocal{O}_{E}
. Then, the maximal integral model of
(V,\varphi)
is given by
h^{-1}\pazocal{O}_{E}
. This is because
\Delta(h^{-1}\pazocal{O}_{E})=k
together with (
4.1
).

Let
T
be an integral model for
(V,\varphi)
and let
r
be its rank as a free
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module. The cokernel of the inclusion
\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)\subset T
is a torsion
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module of finite type and there exists elements
g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}
in
\pazocal{O}_{E}
with
v_{E}(g_{i})\leq v_{E}(g_{i+1})
such that

T/\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)\cong\pazocal{O}_{E}/(g_{1})\oplus\pazocal{O}_{E}/(g_{2})\oplus\cdots\oplus\pazocal{O}_{E}/(g_{r}).

Equivalently, there exists a basis
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})
of
T
over
\pazocal{O}_{E}
such that

\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)=(g_{1})v_{1}\oplus(g_{2})v_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus(g_{r})v_{r}.

The elements
g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}
are unique up to multiplication by units and are called
the elementary divisors relative to the inclusion of
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattices
\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)\subset T
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
t
be a basis of
T
over
\pazocal{O}_{E}
and let
F
be the matrix of
\varphi
written in the bases
\sigma^{*}\textbf{t}
and
t
. The elementary divisors relative to the inclusion
\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)\subset T
are the elementary divisors of the matrix
F
, up to units in
\pazocal{O}_{E}
.

Proof.

If
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
denotes the elementary divisors of
F
with
v_{E}(f_{i})\leq v_{E}(f_{i+1})
, the Smith’s normal form Theorem implies that there exists
U,V\in\operatorname{GL}_{r}(\pazocal{O}_{E})
such that
UF=\operatorname{diag}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})V
. If we let
\textbf{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})
be the basis of
T
corresponding to
V\cdot\mathbf{t}
, this relation reads

\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)=(f_{1})v_{1}\oplus(f_{2})v_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus(f_{r})v_{r}.

By uniqueness of the ideals
(g_{1}),\ldots,(g_{r})
, we conclude that
(f_{i})=(g_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

Let
\widehat{\pazocal{O}}_{E}
denote the completion of
\pazocal{O}_{E}
with respect to its valuation, and let
\widehat{E}
be its field of fractions. The datum of
V\otimes_{E}\widehat{E}
equipped with
\varphi\otimes\sigma
defines a Frobenius space over
\widehat{E}
; let
V_{\widehat{\pazocal{O}}}
be its maximal integral model. Given a matrix
M
with coefficients in
\pazocal{O}_{E}
, the valuation of its elementary divisors are unchanged if we consider
M
in
\widehat{\pazocal{O}}_{E}
. Consequently, one deduces from Lemma
4.4
that the inclusion
V_{\pazocal{O}}\otimes_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\widehat{\pazocal{O}}_{E}\subseteq V_{\widehat{\pazocal{O}}}
is an equality.

Definition 4.6
.

We let the
type of
T
be the sequence
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{r})
of the valuations of the elementary divisors relative to the inclusion
\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)\subset T
ordered such that
0\leq e_{1}\leq e_{2}\leq\cdots\leq e_{r}
. We define
the range
r_{T}
of T
to be the integer
e_{r}
.

Remark 4.7
.

We have
\Delta(T)=e_{1}+\ldots+e_{r}
so that
r\leq\Delta(T)\leq r\cdot r_{T}
where
\Delta(T)
denotes the discriminant of
T
.

We shall denote by
V_{\pazocal{O}}
the maximal integral model of
(V,\varphi)
. The following proposition enables us to say
how far
an integral lattice is from being maximal given its range.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
T
be an
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattice in
V
stable by
\varphi
. Let
s
be a non-negative integer. If the range of
T
satisfies
r_{T}\leq s(q-1)
, then
V_{\pazocal{O}}\subset\varpi^{-s}T
.

We start by a lemma:

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
U
be an
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattice in
V
such that
U\subset\varphi(\sigma^{*}U)
. Then
V_{\pazocal{O}}\subset U
.

Proof.

For
n\geq 0
, we let
\sigma^{n*}:=(\sigma^{n})^{*}
and denote by
\varphi^{n}:\sigma^{n*}V\to V
the
E
-linear morphism given by the composition

\sigma^{n*}V\xrightarrow{\sigma^{(n-1)*}\varphi}\sigma^{(n-1)^{*}}V\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow\sigma^{*}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\longrightarrow}}V.

We consider the following sub-
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module of
V
:

\tilde{U}:=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\varphi^{n}(\sigma^{n*}U).

As an increasing union of
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattices,
\tilde{U}
is a sub-
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module of
V
. The intersection
V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap\tilde{U}
is finitely generated over
\pazocal{O}_{E}
, stable by
\varphi
and generates
V
over
E
as it contains the
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattice
V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap U
. That is,
V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap\tilde{U}
is an integral model whose discriminant

\Delta(V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap\tilde{U}):=\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(\frac{V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap\tilde{U}}{\varphi(\sigma^{*}V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap\tilde{U})}\right)=\operatorname{length}_{\pazocal{O}_{E}}\left(\frac{V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap\tilde{U}}{\varphi(\sigma^{*}V_{\pazocal{O}})\cap\tilde{U}}\right)

is smaller than that of
V{O}
(indeed, any chain of strict submodules from
\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)
to
L
induces, by intersecting with
\tilde{U}
, a smaller chain of strict submodules from
\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)\cap\tilde{U}
to
L\cap\tilde{U}
). By maximality,
\Delta(L)
is minimal hence
V_{\pazocal{O}}=V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap\tilde{U}
. We deduce that there exists a non-negative integer
m
such that
V_{\pazocal{O}}\subset\varphi^{m}(\sigma^{m*}U)
. Because
\varphi(\sigma^{*}V_{\pazocal{O}})\subset V_{\pazocal{O}}
, we have
\sigma^{*}V_{\pazocal{O}}\subset\varphi^{-1}(V_{\pazocal{O}})
and by immediate recursion one gets
\sigma^{m*}V_{\pazocal{O}}\subset\varphi^{-m}(V_{\pazocal{O}})\subset\sigma^{m*}U
. We conclude that
V_{\pazocal{O}}\subset U
because
\sigma:\pazocal{O}_{E}\to\pazocal{O}_{E}
is faithfully flat.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.8
.

Let
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{r})
be the type of
T
. Recall that
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}_{E}
denotes the maximal ideal of
\pazocal{O}_{E}
. There exists a basis
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
of
T
such that
\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)=\mathfrak{p}^{e_{1}}t_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{e_{2}}t_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{e_{r}}t_{r}
. By assumption,
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}\leq s(q-1)
and thus

\displaystyle\varpi^{-s}T

\displaystyle\subset\varpi^{-s}\left(\mathfrak{p}^{e_{1}-s(q-1)}t_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{e_{2}-s(q-1)}t_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{e_{r}-s(q-1)}t_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{p}^{e_{1}-sq}t_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{e_{2}-sq}t_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{e_{r}-sq}t_{r}

\displaystyle=\varpi^{-qs}\varphi(\sigma^{*}T)

\displaystyle=\varphi(\sigma^{*}(\varpi^{-s}T)).

Hence,
U:=\varpi^{-s}T
satisfies
U\subset\varphi(\sigma^{*}U)
and we deduce that
V_{\pazocal{O}}\subset U
by Lemma
4.9
.
∎

Akin to integral models, there is also a notion of
good sublattices
.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
L
be a finitely generated
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-submodule of
V
. We say that
L
is a
good sublattice for
(V,\varphi)
if
\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)=L
. We say that
L
is
maximal
if it is not strictly included in another good sublattice of
(V,\varphi)
.

Proposition 4.11
.

A maximal good sublattice for
(V,\varphi)
exists, is unique and contained in
V_{\pazocal{O}}
.

Proof.

First note that any good sublattice
L
for
(V,\varphi)
is contained in
V_{\pazocal{O}}
: indeed,
L+V_{\pazocal{O}}
is again an integral model, hence included in
V_{\pazocal{O}}
. The sum
U
over all good sublattice for
(V,\varphi)
exists (it is non-empty as the zero module is a good sublattice) and therefore included in
V_{\pazocal{O}}
. Because
\pazocal{O}_{E}
is Noetherian,
U
is a finitely generated
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module. We also have
\varphi(\sigma^{*}U)=U
. We deduce that
U
is maximal, unique and contained in
V_{\pazocal{O}}
.
∎

We shall denote by
V_{\text{good}}
the maximal good sublattice of
(V,\varphi)
; it is a sub-
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module of
V{O}
.

Proposition 4.12
.

The inclusion
V_{\operatorname{good}}\subset V{O}
splits.

Proof.

Consider the subobject of
(V,\varphi)
whose underlying
E
-vector space
U
is generated by
V_{\operatorname{good}}
over
E
. Its maximal integral model is
V_{\operatorname{good}}
(it has zero discriminant) and thus
V_{\pazocal{O}}\cap U\subset V_{\operatorname{good}}
. In particular, the quotient module
V_{\pazocal{O}}/V_{\operatorname{good}}
is finite torsion-free, hence finite free over
\pazocal{O}_{E}
. In particular, the surjection
V_{\pazocal{O}}\to V_{\pazocal{O}}/V_{\operatorname{good}}
splits and the proposition follows.
∎

Definition 4.13
.

We call the rank of
V_{\text{good}}
the
non-degenerate rank of
(V,\varphi)
. We say that
(V,\varphi)
has
good reduction
if
V_{\text{good}}=V_{\pazocal{O}}
;
i.e.
if the non-degenerate rank is maximal.

We can give a rather useful formula for
V_{\operatorname{good}}
in terms of
V_{\pazocal{O}}
.

Lemma 4.14
.

\displaystyle V_{\operatorname{good}}=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}{\varphi^{n}(\sigma^{n*}V_{\pazocal{O}})}
.

Proof.

The right-hand side
L
is a decreasing intersection of
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-lattices hence is itself a finitely generated
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module. Since it further verifies
\varphi(\sigma^{*}L)=L
, one obtains
L\subset V_{\operatorname{good}}
. But
V_{\operatorname{good}}=\varphi^{n}(\sigma^{n*}V_{\operatorname{good}})\subset\varphi^{n}(\sigma^{n*}V_{\pazocal{O}})
for all
n\geq 0
, therefore
L=V_{\operatorname{good}}
.
∎

Maximal good sublattices have an interpretation in terms of
Frobenius sheaves
that we now recall. Let
X
be a smooth connected scheme over
\mathbb{F}
, and let
\pi(X)
be its étale fundamental group. We still denote by
\sigma
the Frobenius on
X
. Let
\pazocal{F}(X)
be the category whose objects are pairs
(\pazocal{V},\varphi)
where
\pazocal{V}
is a locally-free
\pazocal{O}_{X}
-module of finite rank and
\varphi:\sigma^{*}\pazocal{V}\to\pazocal{V}
is an isomorphism of
\pazocal{O}_{X}
-modules. Morphisms in this category are morphisms of the underlying
\pazocal{O}_{X}
-modules with commuting
\varphi
-action.

Example 4.15
.

Objects of
\pazocal{F}(\operatorname{Spec}E)
are Frobenius spaces over
E
, and objects of
\pazocal{F}(\operatorname{Spec}\pazocal{O}_{E})
are pairs
(V,\varphi)
where
V
is a finite free
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-module, and where
\varphi:\sigma^{*}V\to V
is an
\pazocal{O}_{E}
-linear isomorphism.

Until the end of this subsection,
E
is a local function field with perfect residue field
k
. As a consequence,
E=k(\!(\varpi)\!)
. We fix a separable closure
E^{s}
of
E
and denote by
G_{E}
the absolute Galois group
\operatorname{Gal}(E^{s}|E)
of
E
. Let also
I_{E}\subset G_{E}
be the inertia subgroup. The following result is due to Katz in
[
Kat2
, Prop. 4.1.1]
.

Theorem 4.16
.

There is a rank-preserving equivalence of categories from
\pazocal{F}(X)
to the category of
\mathbb{F}
-linear continuous representation of
\pi(X)
, which commutes with base change. For
X=\operatorname{Spec}E
, it is explicitly given by

\underline{V}=(V,\varphi)\longmapsto T\underline{V}=\{x\in V\otimes_{E}E^{s}~|~x=\varphi(\sigma^{*}x)\}

where
\pi(\operatorname{Spec}E)
is identified with
\operatorname{Gal}(E^{s}|E)
, and acts on the right-hand side of
V\otimes_{E}E^{s}
.

The following proposition is almost immediate from Katz’s equivalence:

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
\underline{V}
be a Frobenius space over
E
. The non-degenerate rank of
(V,\varphi)
equals the rank of
(T\underline{V})^{I_{E}}
. In particular,
\underline{V}
has good reduction if and only if
T\underline{V}
is unramified.

Proof.

As
\pi(\operatorname{Spec}\pazocal{O}_{E})\cong G_{E}/I_{E}
, the representation
(T\underline{V})^{I_{E}}
is the maximal subobject of
T\underline{V}
which comes from an object in
\pazocal{F}(\operatorname{Spec}\pazocal{O}_{E})
by Katz’s equivalence. Yet, elements in
\pazocal{F}(\operatorname{Spec}\pazocal{O}_{E})
which specialize to subobjects of
\underline{V}
by base change to
E
are exactly the good sublattices of
\underline{V}
.
∎

We end this subsection by the next result, which will be the main ingredient to obtain Theorem
D
of the introduction.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
\underline{V}=(V,\varphi)
be a Frobenius space over
E
, and let
x\in V
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

There exists
y\in V\otimes_{E}E^{\operatorname{ur}}
such that
x=y-\varphi(\tau^{*}y)
,

(ii)

x\in V_{\operatorname{good}}+(\operatorname{id}_{V}-\varphi)(V)
,

(iii)

x\in V_{\pazocal{O}}+(\operatorname{id}_{V}-\varphi)(V)
.

Proof.

We first prove the equivalence between

(
i
)

and

(

⁢
i
i

)

. Let
\mathbf{1}:\sigma^{*}E\to E
be the canonical
E
-linear isomorphism and let
\mathbbm{1}
be the
neutral
Frobenius space
(E,\mathbf{1})
over
E
. Let also
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{V})
be the
\mathbb{F}
-vector space of Yoneda extensions of
\mathbbm{1}
by
\underline{V}
in the category
\pazocal{F}(\operatorname{Spec}E)
. We have an isomorphism of
\mathbb{F}
-vector spaces, natural in
\underline{V}
,
