L=F_{\mathfrak{p}}
. By Theorem
D
(Theorem
4.62
), there is an inclusion of
A
-modules:

\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})\subseteq\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})_{\ell}.

(5.6)

We strongly suspect the above to be an equality although we were unable to prove it in generality. The following is our expected analogue of Conjecture
(C

4

)
:

Conjecture 5.8
.

The inclusion (
5.6
) is an equality. In particular,
\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{M})_{\ell}
does not depend on
\ell
.

We stated Conjecture
5.8
as the only examples we could produce of extensions which belongs to the right-hand side of (
5.6
), but not in the left-hand one, were not regulated. Still, we owe the reader stronger motivations for it. For the remainder of this section, we present some evidences for Conjecture
5.8
, in Theorems
5.9
and
5.13
below.

Hereafter we assume
A=\mathbb{F}[t]
. Let
\underline{V}=(V,\varphi)
be a Frobenius space over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
(Subsection
4.1
). From
\underline{V}
we obtain an
A
-motive over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
, denoted
A\otimes\underline{V}
, whose underlying module is
A\otimes V
and whose morphisms is
\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\varphi
. Those
A
-motives form a quite restrictive class: observe that
A\otimes\underline{V}
is effective of weight and Hodge weight zero.

Theorem 5.9
.

Assume that
\underline{V}
has good reduction (Definition
4.13
). Then Conjecture
5.8
is true for
A\otimes\underline{V}
.

We will in fact prove a slightly more general version:

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\underline{N}=(N,\tau_{N})
be an
A
-motive over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
such that :

(i)

it is effective,

(ii)

it has good reduction,

(iii)

there exists an
\pazocal{A}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
-lattice
\Lambda
in
N\otimes_{A\otimes F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\pazocal{B}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
such that both

(a)

\tau_{N}(\tau^{*}\Lambda)=\Lambda
,

(b)

As
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
-vector spaces,
N\otimes_{A\otimes F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})=N\oplus\Lambda
.

Then, Conjecture
5.8
is true for
\underline{N}
.

The assumption
A=\mathbb{F}[t]
was superfluous so far; we use it next to relate Proposition
5.10
to Theorem
5.9
in the next lemma.

Lemma 5.11
.

The
A
-motive
A\otimes\underline{V}
satisfies the condition of Proposition
5.10
.

Proof.

Write
\underline{N}=(N,\tau_{N})
for the
A
-motive
A\otimes\underline{V}
. That
\underline{N}
is effective and has good reduction is clear. It remains to find
\Lambda
. Identifying
\pazocal{A}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
with
F_{\mathfrak{p}}[\![t^{-1}]\!]
and
N\subset N_{A\otimes F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\pazocal{B}_{\infty}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
with
V[t]\subset V(\!(t^{-1})\!)
, we can choose
\Lambda=t^{-1}V[\![t^{-1}]\!]
.
∎

We are left with the proof of Proposition
5.10
. First, we show:

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\ell
be a maximal ideal of
A
and let
\underline{N}
be an
A
-motive over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
as in Proposition
5.10
. Then
N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}+(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{N})(N)
is
\ell
-adically closed in
N
.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda
be as in Proposition
5.10

(

⁢
i
i
i

)

. For
n\geq 0
, let
N_{n}
be the finite dimensional
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
-vector space
\ell^{n}\Lambda\cap N
.
(N_{n})_{n}
defines an increasing sequence of subspaces of
N
and we both have
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}N_{n}=N
and
N=N_{n}\oplus\ell^{n}N
. We claim that:

N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap N=(N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap N_{n})\oplus\ell^{n}(N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap N).

To see this, note that since
\underline{N}
has good reduction, the image of
N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap N_{n}
through
N\to N/\ell^{n}N
equals the maximal integral model of
(N/\ell^{n}N,\tau_{M})
. Hence
N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap N\subset(N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap N_{n})\oplus\ell^{n}N
and the claim follows.

Let
m\in N
be such that there exists a sequence
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
in
N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}+(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{N})(N)
which converges
\ell
-adically to
m
. We can assume without loss of generality that
m_{n}\in N_{n}
for all
n
. Yet, we have
m\in N_{d}
for a large enough integer
d
. If
p_{d}
denotes the projection onto
N_{d}
orthogonally to
\ell^{d}N
, we obtain
m=p_{d}(m)=p_{d}(m_{d})\in(N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap N_{d})+(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{N})(N_{d})
as desired.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.10
.

Let
[\underline{E}]=\iota(m)
be an extension in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\text{good}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{N})_{\ell}
. By definition,
m\in N
, and by Proposition
2.29
, there exists
\xi\in(N_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{ur}}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
such that

m=\xi-\tau_{N}(\tau^{*}\xi).

(5.7)

Reducing (
5.7
) modulo
\ell^{n}
for all
n\geq 1
, we obtain from Proposition
4.18
, applied to the Frobenius space
(N/\ell^{n}N,\tau_{N})
, that

\forall n\geq 1:\quad m\in\tilde{L}_{n}+(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{N})(N)+\ell^{n}N

where
\tilde{L}_{n}
is a lift in
N
of a maximal integral model for
(N/\ell^{n}N,\tau_{N})
. It follows from Proposition
4.48
that
m
belongs to the
\ell
-adic closure of
N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}+(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{N})(N)
. But the later is already closed by Lemma
5.12
. It follows that
m\in N_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}+(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{N})(N)
, which amounts to
[\underline{E}]\in\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\text{reg}}_{\pazocal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbbm{1},\underline{N})
as desired.
∎

Let
\underline{M}=\underline{A}(n)
be the
n
th twist of the Carlitz motive over
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
(Example
2.6
). That is,
M=F_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]
and that
\tau_{M}
acts by mapping
\tau^{*}p(t)
to
(t-\theta)^{-n}p(t)^{(1)}
, for
p(t)\in F_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]
. Let
(\ell)\neq\mathfrak{m}
be a maximal ideal of
A=\mathbb{F}[t]
.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
n
be a non negative integer and let
\underline{M}=\underline{A}(n)
. Then Conjecture
5.8
is true for
\underline{M}
.

The original proof of Theorem
5.13
only worked for
n
a power of the characteristic of
\mathbb{F}
. We are thankful to the referee for suggesting this new argument which fills our previous gap.

Proof of Theorem
5.13
.

Let
m\in M+\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}M)
be such that there exists
f\in(M_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{ur}}})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
for which
f-\tau_{M}(\tau^{*}f)=m
. That is,
m=(t-\theta)^{-n}p\in(t-\theta)^{-n}\cdot F_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]
and
f\in F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{ur}}[t]^{\wedge}_{\ell}
. We let
s:=v_{\mathfrak{p}}(p)
be the minimum of the valuation of the coefficients of the polynomial
p
; we have to show that up to subtracting an element of
(\operatorname{id}-\tau_{M})(M)
to
m
,
i.e.
a polynomial of the form
(t-\theta)^{n}h-h^{(1)}
to
p
, we can find
p
with
v_{\mathfrak{p}}(p)\geq 0
. Suppose otherwise, and denote by
s<0
the maximal valuation of such
p
.

From the relation

p=(t-\theta)^{n}f-f^{(1)},

which we consider in
F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{ur}}[t]^{\wedge}_{\ell}=\mathbb{F}(\!(\varpi)\!)^{d}[\![\ell]\!]
where
d=[A/\ell:\mathbb{F}]
, we deduce that the coefficients
(c_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{F}(\!(\varpi)\!)^{d}
of
f
as a formal power series in the variable
\ell
are bounded. In particular, the value
v_{\mathfrak{p}}(f):=\inf_{i}\{v_{\mathfrak{p}}(c_{i})\}
is well-defined and, since
s<0
, satisfies
s=v_{\mathfrak{p}}(f^{(1)})=qv_{\mathfrak{p}}(f)
. This implies that
q
divides
s
, from which one deduces the existence of a polynomial
h
such that
v_{\mathfrak{p}}(p+(t-\theta)^{n}h-h^{(1)})>s
. But this contradicts the maximality of
s
.
∎

Appendix A
Exact categories

Exact categories were introduced by Quillen in
[
Qui1
]
in order to define their
K
-groups. By definition, exact categories possess a class of short sequences satisfying several axioms which are modeled out of properties of exact sequences in abelian category; in that respect, short sequences in those classes are called short exact.

The
embedding theorem
(
cf
.
[
Büh10
, §A]
) states that any exact category which is small embeds fully and faithfully into an abelian category, in such a way that exact sequences coincide. This is presumably the main reason why the literature on this topic does not abound: many of the properties of exact categories can be deduced from their counterpart in abelian categories.

There are, however, some statements involved at certain key steps in the course of this text that we did not find clearly stated in references. Among those,

-

There does not seem to be a consensus on what
long exact sequences
and
higher extension groups
mean in an exact category
\mathscr{C}
. One could either take Yoneda extensions for definition (as we do below), but then it is not immediate that there are long exact sequences of extension groups attached to a short exact sequence. To palliate to this, one could define extension groups after a choice of an embedding
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{A}
into an abelian category by the embedding theorem, and then define extension groups in
\mathscr{C}
to be the ones in
\mathscr{A}
. While this would yield equivalent definitions for degree
1
extensions, it is not clear what degree
i>1
extensions are, nor that they are independent of the abelian embedding.

-

We did not find references for what a
monoidal exact category
means. We define the corresponding notion in this appendix, and show canonical isomorphisms

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(A\otimes X,B)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(A,B\otimes X^{\vee})

whenever
X
is a dualizable object.

Our main reference for what follows is the paper of Bühler
[
Büh10
]
.

A.1
Definitions

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category. We fix a class
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
of sequences
S
of composable arrows in
\mathscr{C}
of the form:

S:\quad 0\longrightarrow X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y\longrightarrow 0

(A.1)

such that
g
is a cokernel of
f
and
f
is a kernel of
g
. Sequences in the class
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
will be called
short exact
. Morphisms
f
(resp.
g
) which are featured in an exact sequence
S
as in (
A.1
) will be called
(
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
)-admissible mono
(resp.
epi
).

The next definition, in a reduced form compared to Quillen’s, is due to Yoneda.

Definition A.1

(Exact category)
.

We say that
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C}))
–or just
\mathscr{C}
for short–is an
exact category
if
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
verifies the following list of axioms:

\operatorname{Ex}1

For all objects
X
of
\mathscr{C}
, the identity morphism
\operatorname{id}_{X}
is an admissible mono (resp. epi).

\operatorname{Ex}2

The class of admissible mono (resp. epi) is stable under composition.

\operatorname{Ex}3

The pushout (resp. pullback) of an admissible mono along a morphism with the same source (resp. target) is representable in
\mathscr{C}
and itself is an admissible mono (resp. epi).

It can be shown that any sequence
S
isomorphic to a sequence in
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
is itself in
\mathscr{C}
. For any pair of objects
(A,B)
of
\mathscr{C}
, the sequence
0\to A\to A\oplus B\to B\to 0
belongs to
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
and is called
the canonical split sequence
. We shall call
S

split
if
S
is isomorphic to the canonical split sequence.

A.2
Exact functor and the embedding theorem

Let
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C}))
and
(\mathscr{D},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{D}))
be exact categories and let
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be an additive functor.

Definition A.2
.

The functor
F
is called
exact
if
F(S)\in\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{D})
for any
S\in\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
.
F
is said to
reflect exactness
if
F
is exact and if
S\in\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
whenever
F(S)\in\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{D})
.

We say that
F

reflects admissible mono
(resp.
epi
) if
f
is admissible mono (resp. epi) if and only if
F(f)
is.

Any abelian category is endowed with the structure of an exact category in an evident way. Conversely, we record the Freyd–Mitchel embedding theorem
[
Büh10
, Thm. A.1]
.

Theorem A.3

(Embedding Theorem)
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C}))
be a small exact category.

(i)

There is an abelian category
\mathscr{A}
and a fully faithful exact functor
i:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{A}
that reflects exactness. Moreover,
\mathscr{C}
is closed under extensions in
\mathscr{A}
.

(ii)

Assume moreover that
\mathscr{C}
is weakly idempotent complete
8
8

8

Recall that an additive is called
weakly idempotent complete
if every retraction admits a kernel (equivalently, any coretraction admits a cokernel)
[
Büh10
, §7]
.
. Then, one can choose
i:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{A}
as in

(
i
)

in such a way that it further reflects admissible epi.

A.3
The group of extensions

Let
(A,B)
be a pair of objects in
\mathscr{C}
and let
i
be a positive integer. By a
degree
i
extension of
A
by
B
we mean a sequence of composable arrows in
\mathscr{C}
of the form

S:0\to B\xrightarrow{f}E_{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{d_{i-1}}E_{i}\xrightarrow{g}A\to 0

(A.2)

such that, for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}
, there exists a factorization of
d_{j}
into
E_{j}\to F_{j+1}\to E_{j+1}
such that the sequences
S_{j}:0\to F_{j}\to E_{j}\to F_{j+1}\to 0
are short exact. If we do not want to make reference to
A
and
B
, we shall call (
A.2
) a (long) exact sequence in
\mathscr{C}
.

Observe that the short exact sequences
S_{j}
are uniquely determined by
S
up to isomorphism. In particular,
F_{i}=A
,
F_{i-1}=\ker(E_{i}\to A)
and, more generally,
F_{j}=\ker(E_{j+1}\to F_{j+1})
.

(Cup product)

A degree
i
extension
S
of
A
by
B
and a degree
j
extension
T
of
B
by
C

compose
into a degree
i+j
extension of
A
by
C
via the
cup product
:

T\cup S:0\to C\to F_{1}\to\cdots\to F_{j}\xrightarrow{u}E_{1}\to\cdots\to E_{i}\to A\to 0

where we wrote
T:0\to C\to F_{1}\to\cdots\to F_{j}\to B\to 0
and where
u
is the composition of
F_{j}\to B\to E_{1}
. By definition, a degree
i
extension
S
can be written uniquely as the iterative cup product of degree
1
extensions
S=\cup_{j}S_{j}
.

(Equivalence)

Given another extension
S^{\prime}
of
A
by
B
of the same degree, we write
S\equiv S^{\prime}
if there is a commutative diagram in
\mathscr{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

More generally, we shall say that
A
and
B

are equivalent
and write
S\equiv S^{\prime}
if they are equivalent for the equivalence relation generated
9
9

9

That is,
S
is equivalent to
S^{\prime}
if and only if there exists a sequence
S^{\prime\prime}
such that
S\equiv S^{\prime\prime}
and
S^{\prime}\equiv S^{\prime\prime}
. In fact, one could show that, in the case
i=1
,
\equiv
already is an equivalence relation as a version of the five lemma holds in exact categories
[
Büh10
, Lem. 8.9]
.
by
\equiv
. One verifies that cup products are preserved under equivalences:
T^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime}\equiv T\cup S
whenever
T^{\prime}\equiv T
and
S^{\prime}\equiv S
.

The extensions
0\to B\to B\oplus A\to A\to 0
for
i=1
and
0\to B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{id}}}{{\to}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\to}}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{id}}}{{\to}}A\to 0
for
i>1
are called
canonically split extension of degree
i
. A degree
i
extension
S
is called
split
if it is equivalent to the canonically split extension of degree
i
. One verifies that
S\cup T
is split whenever
S
or
T
is.

(Pullback and Pushforward)

Consider two morphisms
a:A^{\prime}\to A
and
b:B^{\prime}\to B
in
\mathscr{C}
. By

Ex
3

, the pullback
E\times_{A}A^{\prime}
and the pushout
B^{\prime}\sqcup_{B}E
exist and their universal property yield that the canonical maps
B\to E\times_{A}A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}\sqcup_{B}E\to A
are a kernel and a cokernel of
E\times_{A}A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}\sqcup_{B}E
respectively. Therefore, for any short exact sequence
S:0\to B\to E\to A\to 0
, we obtain short exact sequences:

\displaystyle a^{*}S:

\displaystyle\quad 0\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow E\times_{A}A^{\prime}\longrightarrow A^{\prime}\longrightarrow 0,

\displaystyle b_{*}S:

\displaystyle\quad 0\longrightarrow B^{\prime}\longrightarrow B^{\prime}\sqcup_{B}E\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0,

which are respectively degree
1
extensions of
A^{\prime}
by
B
and
A
by
B^{\prime}
. We call
a^{*}S
and
b_{*}S
the
pullback of
S
by
a
and the
pushout of
S
by
b
. Pullbacks are immediately extended to degree
i>1
extensions
S
by first writing it as a splitting
S^{\prime}\cup S_{i}
where
S_{i}
(resp
S^{\prime}
) is a degree
1
(resp. degree
i-1
) extension, then by setting
a^{*}S
to be
S^{\prime}\cup a^{*}S_{i}
. Dually for pushforwards.

We leave the following list of facts without proof.

(a)

A pullback or pushout of a split sequence is itself split.

(b)

We have
\operatorname{id}_{A}^{*}S\equiv S
and
\operatorname{id}_{B*}S\equiv S
.

(c)

If
S\equiv S^{\prime}
, then
a^{*}S\equiv a^{*}S^{\prime}
and
b_{*}S\equiv b_{*}S^{\prime}
.

(d)

If
a^{\prime}:A^{\prime\prime}\to A^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\to B^{\prime\prime}
, then
(a^{\prime}\circ a)^{*}S\equiv a^{\prime*}(a^{*}S)
and
(b\circ b^{\prime})^{*}S\equiv b^{*}(b^{\prime*}S)
.

(e)

Given
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
an exact functor,
F(S^{\prime})\equiv F(S)
whenever
S^{\prime}\equiv S
.

(f)

Given
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
an exact functor,
F(a)^{*}F(S)\equiv F(a^{*}S)
and
F(b)_{*}F(S)\equiv F(b_{*}S)
.

(g)

We have
b_{*}(a^{*}S)\equiv a^{*}(b_{*}S)
as extensions of
A^{\prime}
by
B^{\prime}
.

(h)

The sequences
f_{*}S
and
g^{*}S
are split, where
f
and
g
are as in (
A.2
).

(Extension groups)

We assume that
\mathscr{C}
is small. As a consequence, equivalence classes of degree
i
extensions of
A
by
B
form a set, pointed by the equivalence class of the split extension, which we denote by:

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B).

The cup product defines a map of pointed set
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)\times\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\mathscr{C}}(B,C)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{i+j}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,C)
. Proprieties

(
a
)

–

(
d
)

ensure that
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}
defines a bifunctor
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathbf{Set}_{*}
of pointed sets. By

(
e
)

, an exact functor
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
induces a natural transformation of bifunctors of pointed sets
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(F):\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,-)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(F(-),F(-))
.

This construction can be refined into a bifunctor of abelian groups under the
Baer sum
. If
\nabla_{A}:A\to A\oplus A
denotes the diagonal embedding and
\Delta_{B}:B\oplus B\to B
the addition, the
degree
i
Baer sum
is defined as the composition:

+:\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)\times\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A\oplus A,B\oplus B)\xrightarrow{\nabla_{A}^{*}\circ\Delta_{B*}}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B).

The first map corresponds to direct sums of exact sequences, which are also exact
[
Büh10
, Prop. 2.9]
. Then,
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)
becomes an abelian group under
+
with the class of the split exact sequence as the identity. The cup product, as well as
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(F)
, become morphisms of abelian groups in virtue of properties

(
e
)

-

(
f
)

. If the category
\mathscr{C}
is
R
-linear for some commutative ring
R
, then
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)
becomes an
R
-module with
r\in R
acting as pullback by the multiplication by
r
on
A
.

A.4
Long exact sequence of Extensions groups

We again assume that
\mathscr{C}
is small. Consider a short exact sequence
S:0\to X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\to 0
. Let also
A
be an object of
\mathscr{C}
. Then, one can produce two natural complexes of abelian groups.

The

S
and

:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,Z)}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ-}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mapsto S\cup T}

and the

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\mapsto h_{*}S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mapsto S\cup T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mapsto S\cup T}

That two successive arrows in these sequences compose to zero follows easily from properties

(
a
)

–

(
h
)

above.

Proposition A.4
.

Assume that the functor
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,-)
transforms admissible epi into surjections. Then, the functors
\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,-)
are identically zero for
n>i
. Dually, if
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,B)
transforms admissible mono into surjections, the functors
\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,B)
are identically zero for
n>i
.

Proof.

The class of any extension
U
in
\operatorname{Ext}^{i+1}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)
is decomposed as the cup product of a degree one extension
S
and a degree
i
extension
T
whose class are in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{C}}(E,B)
and
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,E)
respectively for some object
E
. The pushout sequence associated to
S
and
A
together with the surjectivity of
g_{*}
imply that the map

S\cup-:\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,E)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{i+1}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)

starred in the pushout sequence, is zero. Since
U
is in its image, its class is zero. Therefore,
\operatorname{Ext}^{i+1}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)=(0)
.

For the general case, observe similarly that a degree
n>i+1
extension
U
decomposes as the cup product of a degree
i+1
and
n-(i+1)
extensions respectively. The class of the first is zero, hence so is the class of
U
.
∎

The author is not aware of a reference that states the exactness of the pushout and pullback long sequences at this level of generality. However, we prove under reasonable assumptions that they are.

Proposition A.5
.

Assume that
\mathscr{C}
is a small exact category that is weakly idempotent complete. Then pushout and pullback sequences are exact.

We begin with a lemma.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
h:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be an embedding of
\mathscr{C}
into an exact category
\mathscr{D}
which reflects exactness and admissible epi. Let
S:0\to X_{n}\to X_{n-1}\to\cdots\to X_{1}\to 0
be a sequence in
\mathscr{C}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

S
is exact,

(ii)

h(S)
is exact in
\mathscr{D}
.

Proof.

If
S
is exact, then
S=\cup_{i}S_{i}
for short exact sequences
S_{i}
, hence
h(S)=\cup_{i}h(S_{i})
. Because
h
reflects exactness, this proves

(
i
)

\Longrightarrow

(

⁢
i
i

)

.

We prove the converse by induction on the length
n
of the sequence; for
n\leq 3
, there is nothing to prove. For
n>3
, suppose
h(S)
is exact. We splice up
h(S)
into exact sequences as

\left(0\to h(X_{n})\to\cdots\to h(X_{2})\to Z\to 0\right)\cup\left(0\to Z\to h(X_{1})\xrightarrow{h(d_{1})}h(X_{0})\to 0\right).

(A.3)

In particular
h(d_{1})
is an admissible epi and so is
d_{1}
by reflection. Hence
d_{1}
admits a kernel
Y_{1}
in
\mathscr{C}
and the sequence
V:0\to Y_{1}\to X_{1}\to X_{0}\to 0
is short exact. By universality of the kernel, the splicing (
A.3
) corresponds to
h(S)=h(U)\cup h(V)
for some sequence
U
in
\mathscr{C}
:

U:0\to X_{n}\xrightarrow{d_{n}}X_{n-1}\to\cdots\to X_{2}\to Y_{1}\to 0.

Since
h(U)
is exact, so is
U
by induction hypothesis. Since
V
is exact, so is their cup product
U\cup V=S
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.5
.

It suffices to apply the statement of Lemma
A.6
to the functor
i:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{A}
produced by the Freyd–Mitchell embedding in

(

⁢
i
i

)

. In particular, the pushout and pullback long sequences become the respective ones for the abelian category
\mathscr{A}
for which the statement is known.
∎

A.5
Extensions and adjunctions

Let
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C}))
and
(\mathscr{D},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{D}))
be exact categories. Let
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be an exact functor that admits a right-adjoint
G:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
which we assume to be exact as well (it is a priori unclear that this holds). We denote by
\eta:\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{D}}\to GF
and
\varepsilon:FG\to\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{C}}
the unit and counit of this adjunction. In particular, given any objects
A
,
B
of
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
respectively, there are maps of groups:

\displaystyle g:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(A),B)\xrightarrow{G}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(GF(A),G(B))\xrightarrow{\eta^{*}_{A}}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,G(B)),

\displaystyle f:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,G(B))\xrightarrow{F}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(A),FG(B))\xrightarrow{(\varepsilon_{B})_{*}}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(A),B).

Proposition A.7
.

The maps
f
and
g
are mutually inverse. In particular, for all
i>0
, there are isomorphisms of groups:

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(A),B)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,G(B)).

Proof.

We only prove that
f\circ g
is equivalent to the identity as the dual statement is proven along the same lines. We consider an extension in
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(A),B)
represented by a long exact sequence
S:0\to B\to E_{1}\to\cdots\to E_{i}\to F(A)\to 0
. We have the following commutative diagram, whose rows are exact by assumption:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG(E_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG(E_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FGF(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG(E_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces**}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{E_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{F(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\eta_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

Above, we denoted by
*
the pullback of
G(E_{i})\to GF(A)\leftarrow A
and by
**
the pushforward of
B\leftarrow FG(B)\rightarrow FG(E_{1})
, as indicated by the symbols
\urcorner
and
\ulcorner
.

This diagram corresponds to
S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\leftarrow}}FG(S)\leftarrow F(g(S))\rightarrow f(g(S))
vertically. Therefore, it suffices to show that the bottom row is equivalent to the first one.

By commutativity of the square
\square
and universal property of the pushforward, there exists a map
s:**\to E_{1}
whose composition with
FG(E_{1})\to**
is the map
\varepsilon_{E_{1}}
and whose composition with
B\to**
is
B\to E_{1}
starred in
S
. In particular, we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces**}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG(E_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{E_{i}}\circ F(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{F(A)}\circ F(\eta_{A})}

To conclude that the above diagram is an equivalence among
f(g(S))
and
S
, it remains to show that
\varepsilon_{F(A)}\circ F(\eta_{A})=\operatorname{id}_{F(A)}
. This results from diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(A),FGF(A))\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}}(FGF(A),F(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(A),F(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,GF(A))\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(GF(A),GF(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,GF(A))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times\operatorname{ad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ad}}

which commutes, as the adjunction maps
\operatorname{ad}
are obtained by composing
F
with the natural transformation
FG\to\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{D}}
. It remains to apply the commutativity of the above square to the couple
(\eta_{A},\operatorname{id}_{GF(A)})
sitting in the lower left corner. Via the lower path, it is mapped to
\eta_{A}
and then to
\operatorname{id}_{F(A)}
. Via the upper path, to
(F(\eta_{A}),\varepsilon_{F(A)})
, then to
\varepsilon_{F(A)}\circ F(\eta_{A})
. Hence
\varepsilon_{F(A)}\circ F(\eta_{A})=\operatorname{id}_{F(A)}
as announced.
∎

A.6
Construction of exact categories

We present a way of constructing exact categories out of certain functors. This procedure will be used in the sequel to endow the category of
A
-motives with the structure of an exact category (
cf
. Definition
2.14
). Let
(\mathscr{D},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{D}))
be an exact category and let
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be an additive functor.

Proposition A.8
.

Assume the following:

(i)

Given
g:Y\to Z
be a morphism in
\mathscr{C}
, if
F(g)
is an admissible epi of
\mathscr{D}
, then
g
admits a kernel
\ker g\to Y
in
\mathscr{C}
and
F(\ker g\to Y)
is a kernel of
F(g)
.

(ii)

Given
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathscr{C}
, if
F(f)
is an admissible mono of
\mathscr{D}
, then
f
admits a cokernel
Y\to\operatorname{coker}f
in
\mathscr{C}
and
F(Y\to\operatorname{coker}f)
is a cokernel of
F(f)
.

Then there exists a unique structure of exact category on
\mathscr{C}
making
F
a functor which reflects exactness. In fact,
F
further reflects admissible epi and mono.

Proof.

Since
F
has to reflect exactness, the candidate class is uniquely determined to be the class
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
of short sequences
S:0\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}Z\to 0
such that
f
is a kernel of
g
,
g
is a cokernel of
f
, and
F(S)
is exact in
\mathscr{D}
.

Assumptions

(
i
)

and

(

⁢
i
i

)

ensure that for short sequences of
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
the former non trivial arrow is a kernel of the latter, and dually. It remains to show the axioms of Definition
2.14
. Axioms

Ex
1

and

Ex
2

are clear as
F
is a functor. To prove

Ex
3

, we fix a short sequence
S:0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
in
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
and a morphism
Z^{\prime}\to Z
. We claim that the pullback
Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}
is representable in
\mathscr{C}
; indeed,
F(Y)\times_{F(Z)}F(Z^{\prime})
is representable in
\mathscr{D}
and hence
F(Y)\oplus F(Z^{\prime})=F(Y\oplus Z^{\prime})\to F(Z)
admits a kernel. By the
obscure axioms
in
\mathscr{D}

[
Büh10
, Prop. 2.16]
, it is an admissible epi since the composition
F(Y)\to F(Y\oplus Z^{\prime})\to F(Z)
is. Hence,
Y\oplus Z^{\prime}\to Z
admits a kernel by

(
i
)

–this kernel represents the pullback
Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}
–and we have
F(Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime})\cong F(Y)\times_{F(Z)}F(Z^{\prime})
.

In particular, by

Ex
3

for
\mathscr{D}
,
F(Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}\to Z)
is an admissible epi, hence
Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}\to Z
admits a kernel in
\mathscr{C}
by

(
i
)

and
\ker F(Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}\to Z)\cong F(\ker(Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}\to Z))
. That is, the sequence

F(0\to\ker(Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}\to Z)\to Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}\to Z\to 0)

belongs to
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{D})
, hence
0\to\ker(Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}\to Z)\to Y\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}\to Z\to 0
is in
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
. The dual statement follows similarly.

That
F
further reflects admissible mono and epi is clear from the class
\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C})
.
∎

A.7
Monoidal exact categories

Let
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C}))
be an exact category and let
\otimes:\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
be a bifunctor making
(\mathscr{C},\otimes)
an additive monoidal category
[
Stack
, 0FNA]
. We denote by
\mathbbm{1}
a neutral object for
\otimes
.

Definition A.9
.

The datum of
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{ex}(\mathscr{C}),\otimes)
is called a
monoidal exact category
if, given any object
X
of
\mathscr{C}
, the endofunctors
-\otimes X
and
X\otimes-
are exact.

It is immediate that, given any couple of objects
(A,B)
in
\mathscr{C}
, the functor
-\otimes X
determines group morphisms

-\otimes X:\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A\otimes X,B\otimes X),\quad[S]\longrightarrow[S\otimes X].

(A.4)

Recall that
X
is called
dualizable
if the functor
-\otimes X
admits a right-adjoint, denoted by
\mathscr{H}\!\operatorname{om}(X,-)
. We denote by
X^{\vee}
the object
\mathscr{H}\!\operatorname{om}(X,\mathbbm{1})
, called
the dual of
X
. Since
\mathscr{H}\!\operatorname{om}(X,-)\cong X^{\vee}\otimes-
, the functor
\mathscr{H}\!\operatorname{om}(X,-)
is exact. In particular, as a corollary of Proposition
A.7
, we obtain:

Corollary A.10
.

Let
X
be a dualizable object of
\mathscr{C}
. Then, there are canonical isomorphisms of groups

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A\otimes X,B)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B\otimes X^{\vee}).
